LAYER 7, THE SYNAPTIC AND JUNCTIONAL LAYER: THE TYPES

The Synaptics of the Human Nervous and Endocrine System

A Complete, Comprehensive, and Thorough Manuscript

Companion volume to the Layer 6 catalogues (301 neuron types; 85 non-neuronal cell types), the Layer 8 catalogue (71 microcircuit types), and the Layer 9 manuscript, in the Certified_Neuroscientist corpus of D. R. Dison, Founder, Artificial Intelligence University (AIU).

Date of compilation: August 9, 2026.


INTRODUCTION: THE LAYER OF THE RELATION

Layer 7 is the corpus's one relation-layer.

Its unit is not a cell and not a circuit but the junction between cells: an entity assembled from parts owned by two different cells - a presynaptic element from one, a postsynaptic element from another, and in the gap junction two half-channels each useless alone.

Its defining state variable, the weight, belongs to the relationship and to neither partner - and the adjustment of that weight is the physical act of learning.

This volume catalogues the junction types: sixty-four of them, each given a full chapter under a template designed for relations rather than objects.

Each chapter answers the four questions only a relation can be asked.

• Who stands on each side - the Layer 6 cast, cited to the corpus catalogues, with each partner's personality and its share of the build.

• What is the machinery - the Layer 4 and 5 inventory of vesicles, sensors, receptors, scaffolds, and connexins.

• What is the transfer function - sign, strength, kinetics, short-term dynamics, and the learning rules the junction supports or proudly refuses.

• What is it the fabric of - the Layer 8 motifs this junction builds, and the Layer 9 addresses where it is installed.

Each chapter runs through twenty-three fixed aspects, from WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT TRANSMITS to WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE, including a SYSTEMS INTERFACE block in the publish-subscribe discipline and a ENGINEERING NOTE proposing the junction as a typed connection in a message-passing architecture - because in engineering terms this volume is the catalogue of EDGE TYPES, as the Layer 6 volumes were the catalogue of nodes and the Layer 8 volume the catalogue of subgraphs.

Two tags divide the roster, as throughout the corpus.

• (COG) marks a junction whose transmission or plasticity participates in cognition; every (COG) chapter closes with ENGLISH READABLE CODE and a runnable Python CODE SAMPLE of its signature computation.

• (NON-COG) marks a junction serving substrate or effector roles; its code sections collapse to a single stated sentence.

A note on the classification, repeated from the outline: the parts of this volume are axes as much as bins, and one physical synapse in tissue is typically several chapters at once - a cortical excitatory contact is Chapter 1 by messenger, Chapter 17 by geometry, and Chapter 53 by its glial wrapping.

Each chapter treats the pure type along its axis and cross-references its usual companions.

The plan of the volume follows the outline that preceded it.

• Part One: the chemical synapse, by messenger system.

• Part Two: the synapse by geometry and target.

• Part Three: the giant and named synapses.

• Part Four: the electrical and mixed junctions.

• Part Five: the plasticity-defined and life-cycle types.

• Part Six: the neuroeffector and neurosecretory junctions.

• Part Seven: the glial and non-neuronal synapses.

• Part Eight: the endocrine junctional equivalents.

• The Cognitive Scoreboard.

One sentence governs everything that follows: the weight belongs to the relationship.


PART ONE. THE CHEMICAL SYNAPSE, BY MESSENGER SYSTEM

Every thought you have ever had was carried, at some point, by a molecule crossing a gap twenty nanometres wide.

Part One catalogues the classical chemical synapse in its messenger varieties: a presynaptic terminal packaging a transmitter into vesicles, a cleft, and a postsynaptic membrane that decides what the molecule means.

The deepest lesson of this part is that the messenger does not decide the message - the receptor does, and the same molecule read by an ion channel is a millisecond command while read by a G-protein cascade it is a minutes-long change of mood.

The first five entries make the point with only two molecules: glutamate read three ways, and gamma-aminobutyric acid read two ways, together account for the overwhelming majority of all synapses in the human brain.

These five are also where the weight lives, for they are the principal adjustable connections of the cognitive layers, and their plasticity rules are the physical form of learning.

Read them as the alphabet of the relation-layer, from which the later parts build geometry, giants, and glia.


CHAPTER 1. THE GLUTAMATERGIC FAST EXCITATORY SYNAPSE (AMPA and NMDA Receptor Synapse) (The Workhorse-and-Teacher Contact) (COG)

Take any cubic millimetre of your cortex and most of the hundred million or so synapses in it are this one design: a bouton full of glutamate facing a spine head studded with two kinds of receptor.

One receptor kind does the work, answering every release with a fast current; the other does the teaching, opening only when the message and the listener's own excitement coincide.

That coincidence rule, discovered in the N-methyl-D-aspartate receptor's voltage-dependent magnesium block, is the closest thing biology has to a printed learning law.

This is the majority synapse of the central nervous system and the principal site of long-term potentiation, which is to say the principal site of memory.

Workhorse names the alpha-amino-3-hydroxy-5-methyl-4-isoxazolepropionic acid receptor, which carries the everyday current.

Teacher names the N-methyl-D-aspartate receptor, which detects coincidences and admits the calcium that changes the weight.

Contact marks it as a classical chemical synapse.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT TRANSMITS:

A chemical synapse releasing glutamate, the brain's standard excitatory amino acid transmitter, onto ionotropic receptors - receptors that are themselves ion channels - producing a depolarising current within a millisecond.

It transmits two things at once: a fast analogue current proportional to the weight, and, when pre- and postsynaptic activity coincide, a calcium signal that rewrites that weight.

THE JUNCTIONAL ARCHITECTURE:

A presynaptic bouton, owned by the sending axon, holding one or a few active zones with docked vesicles; a cleft of about 20 to 24 nanometres, a nanometre being a millionth of a millimetre; and a postsynaptic density, owned by the receiving cell, typically on a dendritic spine head.

This is the classic Gray type I asymmetric synapse, so called because the postsynaptic density is visibly thicker than the presynaptic side under the electron microscope.

Emerging evidence shows release sites and receptor clusters aligned in trans-synaptic nanocolumns, so that the two cells co-own even the alignment of the machine.

THE MOLECULAR MACHINERY:

Small clear vesicles of about 40 nanometres filled by the vesicular glutamate transporters VGLUT1 or VGLUT2; the SNARE fusion proteins - soluble N-ethylmaleimide-sensitive factor attachment protein receptors - synaptobrevin, syntaxin-1, and SNAP-25; and synaptotagmin-1 as the fast calcium sensor.

Postsynaptically, alpha-amino-3-hydroxy-5-methyl-4-isoxazolepropionic acid (AMPA) receptors built from the subunits GluA1 to GluA4, N-methyl-D-aspartate (NMDA) receptors built from two GluN1 plus two GluN2 subunits (GluN2A or GluN2B in the forebrain), and the scaffold protein PSD-95 holding perhaps a hundred receptors in register with adhesion proteins such as neuroligin and neurexin.

THE PARTNERS (THE LAYER 6 CAST):

The presynaptic side is any glutamatergic principal neuron of the Layer 6 catalogue - the intratelencephalic and extratelencephalic pyramidal families, thalamic relay cells, granule cells, and the excitatory cells of the spinal dorsal horn.

The postsynaptic side is almost everything: other principal cells on their spines, and every interneuron class on its shafts, so the same design serves the context-weaving neuron of the Layer 6 volume and the basket cell that silences it.

An astrocyte process usually wraps the cleft, previewing the tripartite entry of Part Seven.

SIZE AND SCALE:

The bouton is roughly 0.5 to 1 micrometre across, the active zone about 0.2 to 0.4 micrometres, and the postsynaptic density about 0.3 micrometres wide and 30 to 50 nanometres thick.

A typical cortical contact holds 20 to 100 AMPA receptors and 10 to 20 NMDA receptors, and receptor count scales linearly with spine head volume - the anatomy of the weight.

NUMBER AND DISTRIBUTION:

On the order of 10 to the power 14 synapses in the human brain, of which roughly 80 to 90 percent in cortex are excitatory glutamatergic contacts of this design - the most numerous junction type in this catalogue by a wide margin.

Cortex, hippocampus, striatum, thalamus, cerebellar cortex, brainstem, spinal cord: wherever there are neurons, the majority of contacts are this one.

WHERE IT IS INSTALLED (THE LAYER 9 MAP):

• Neocortex, where it is the fabric of every feedforward and recurrent excitatory pathway.

• Hippocampal formation, where the Schaffer collateral contact onto CA1 is the most studied synapse on Earth and the reference preparation for long-term potentiation.

• Striatum, where corticostriatal and thalamostriatal terminals deliver the candidate actions that dopamine will judge.

• Thalamus, where retinal, lemniscal, and layer 5 driver terminals carry the ascending senses.

• Cerebellar cortex, as the mossy fibre and parallel fibre systems of the Part Three giants.

• Brainstem and spinal cord, where primary afferents and descending motor commands terminate in this design.

• Amygdala, where fear learning is stored in the potentiation of these contacts.

THE TRANSFER FUNCTION:

Sign positive: one presynaptic spike releases zero or more vesicles, each producing a quantal AMPA conductance of about 0.2 to 1 nanosiemens that drives the membrane toward about 0 millivolts.

The AMPA component is approximately linear in voltage; the NMDA component is coincidence-gated, because extracellular magnesium blocks the open channel at rest and is expelled only by depolarisation, following a sigmoid unblock function of voltage.

The junction therefore computes a multiplication: NMDA current is roughly (presynaptic release) times (postsynaptic depolarisation), and that product, delivered as calcium, is the plasticity signal.

TEMPORAL SIGNATURE:

AMPA current rises in about 0.2 milliseconds and decays with a time constant of about 1 to 3 milliseconds; single-channel conductance is about 8 to 10 picosiemens, with 25 to 50 channels open per quantum.

NMDA current rises in about 10 milliseconds and decays over about 50 to 100 milliseconds for GluN2A-rich receptors and 200 milliseconds or more for GluN2B-rich receptors, with a single-channel conductance of about 50 picosiemens.

Synaptic delay from presynaptic spike to postsynaptic current is 0.5 to 1 millisecond.

SHORT-TERM DYNAMICS:

Both facilitation and depression occur, set by release probability: high-probability contacts (probability around 0.5 to 0.9) depress over tens to hundreds of milliseconds as the docked pool drains, while low-probability contacts (around 0.1 to 0.3) facilitate as residual calcium accumulates, with facilitation decaying over roughly 50 to 300 milliseconds.

Cortical pyramid-to-pyramid contacts are mildly depressing on average; recovery from depression takes roughly 0.5 to 1 second.

LONG-TERM PLASTICITY:

This is the flagship learning synapse: NMDA-receptor-dependent long-term potentiation adds AMPA receptors and enlarges the spine, long-term depression removes them, and spike-timing-dependent plasticity potentiates when the presynaptic spike leads the postsynaptic spike by up to about 20 milliseconds and depresses for the reverse order.

The changes persist from hours to, with protein synthesis, the lifetime of the memory, and the silent synapse of Part Five is this junction's blank starting state.

RELIABILITY AND NOISE:

A single cortical contact is unreliable by design: release probability commonly 0.1 to 0.5, quantal content often a single vesicle, and failure rates of 50 percent or more at individual contacts.

Reliability is achieved statistically, by the convergence of thousands of contacts per receiving cell, which is what makes each individual weight cheap to adjust.

ENERGY BUDGET:

The most expensive signalling element in the brain: postsynaptic ion pumping to reverse the currents of glutamatergic transmission accounts for roughly a third to a half of cortical signalling energy, on the order of 10 to the power 4 to 10 to the power 5 molecules of adenosine triphosphate per vesicle released and its postsynaptic consequences.

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY:

Contacts begin as filopodial touches, assemble scaffold and NMDA receptors first, and often spend infancy as silent NMDA-only synapses before activity inserts AMPA receptors.

A developmental subunit switch from GluN2B toward GluN2A shortens the teaching signal as circuits mature, and massive overproduction is followed by activity-dependent pruning into adolescence.

EVOLUTIONARY PROVENANCE:

Ionotropic glutamate receptors predate nervous systems - relatives exist in plants and bacteria - and the AMPA-plus-NMDA pairing with its magnesium-block learning rule is ancient in animals, being present in cnidarians and refined, not invented, by vertebrates.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The wrapping astrocyte clears glutamate within a millisecond through the excitatory amino acid transporters EAAT1 and EAAT2, recycles it as glutamine, and supplies the co-agonist D-serine that the NMDA receptor requires alongside glutamate.

Microglia patrol and prune the weakest of these contacts, completing the life-cycle loop of Part Five.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS MICROCIRCUITS:

This junction is the fabric of nearly every excitatory motif in the Layer 8 catalogue: the feedforward chain, the recurrent attractor, the winner-take-all competition, and the autoassociative memory all store their function in these weights.

Remove it and the microcircuit catalogue collapses to its inhibitory skeleton: nothing would drive, nothing would associate, and nothing would learn.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS:

Anti-NMDA-receptor encephalitis, an autoimmune removal of the teacher receptor, produces psychosis, seizures, and catatonia; excess glutamate in stroke and traumatic injury kills cells through NMDA-mediated calcium excitotoxicity.

GRIN2A and GRIN2B mutations cause epilepsy and intellectual disability, ketamine and phencyclidine act by blocking the NMDA channel, and hypofunction of this receptor is a leading hypothesis for schizophrenia.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• SUBSCRIBES (INPUTS).

◦ Presynaptic spike train, 0 to about 100 hertz.

◦ Postsynaptic membrane voltage, minus 80 to 0 millivolts, read by the magnesium block.

◦ Astrocytic D-serine tone and ambient magnesium about 1 millimolar.

• PUBLISHES (OUTPUTS).

◦ AMPA current, quantal conductance 0.2 to 1 nanosiemens, reversal about 0 millivolts.

◦ NMDA calcium flux into the spine, about 10 percent of NMDA current.

• STATE.

◦ The weight: AMPA receptor count 0 to about 150, plus spine volume as its structural shadow.

◦ Plasticity variables: spine calcium, calcium/calmodulin-dependent protein kinase II activation, readily releasable vesicle pool 0 to about 10.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Release probability 0.1 to 0.9; cleft width 20 to 24 nanometres; NMDA to AMPA ratio about 0.3 to 0.5.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ AMPA decay 1 to 3 milliseconds; NMDA decay 50 to 200 milliseconds; facilitation 50 to 300 milliseconds; depression recovery 0.5 to 1 second; long-term potentiation expression minutes to years.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Anti-NMDA-receptor encephalitis; excitotoxic cell death in stroke; GRIN2A and GRIN2B epileptic encephalopathies; runaway potentiation as a seizure substrate.

THE ENGINEERING NOTE:

Propose the connection type fast_excitatory_learning_connection.

A message on this connection carries a spike timestamp and is multiplied by a stored weight; the connection additionally exposes a plasticity port that reads the destination's activity.

The nearest artificial intelligence analogue is the trainable weighted edge of a neural network, with the biological difference that the learning rule is local and physical - the gradient is not backpropagated but computed at the junction itself by a magnesium ion.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to decide whether a synapse strengthens, using the N-methyl-D-aspartate receptor as a coincidence detector with a voltage-dependent magnesium unblock.
Create a number variable called membrane voltage with initial value minus 70.
Create a number variable called glutamate bound with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called magnesium unblock with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called calcium entry with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called weight change with initial value 0.
If a presynaptic spike arrives is true, then perform the following actions.
    Set glutamate bound to 1.
End the conditional block.
If a postsynaptic spike arrives is true, then perform the following actions.
    Set membrane voltage to 0.
End the conditional block.
Calculate the difference of membrane voltage and minus 70, then divide the result by 70.
Set magnesium unblock to the result.
Calculate the product of glutamate bound and magnesium unblock.
Set calcium entry to the result.
If calcium entry is greater than 0.5 is true, then perform the following actions.
    Set weight change to 1.
Otherwise, perform the following actions.
    Set weight change to 0.
End the conditional block.
Display weight change to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the coincidence rule is one multiplication inside one truth table, and Python states both without ceremony.

# NMDA coincidence detection: glutamate binding AND depolarisation
# are both required, because magnesium blocks the open channel at rest
# and is expelled only by depolarisation (Jahr & Stevens, J Neurosci 1990).
import math

def mg_unblock(v_mv, mg_mM=1.0):
    # Sigmoid voltage dependence of magnesium unblock.
    return 1.0 / (1.0 + (mg_mM / 3.57) * math.exp(-0.062 * v_mv))

def nmda_calcium(glu_bound, v_mv):
    g_nmda = 1.0                       # normalised conductance when bound
    drive = 0.0 - v_mv                 # driving force toward 0 mV reversal
    return glu_bound * g_nmda * mg_unblock(v_mv) * drive / 70.0

print("pre  post  V(mV)  unblock  Ca-signal  potentiate")
for pre in (0, 1):
    for post in (0, 1):
        v = 0.0 if post else -70.0     # postsynaptic spike depolarises
        ca = nmda_calcium(pre, v)
        print(f"{pre}    {post}   {v:6.0f}  {mg_unblock(v):7.3f}  "
              f"{ca:9.3f}  {ca > 0.5}")
# Only the (1, 1) row potentiates: a physical AND gate, hence a local
# Hebbian learning rule with no external teacher.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The only junction in this catalogue that is simultaneously the most numerous synapse in the brain and a two-channel machine whose second channel is an AND gate: an asymmetric contact on a spine whose fast current does the thinking's work and whose magnesium-gated calcium current does the thinking's learning.


CHAPTER 2. THE KAINATE-RECEPTOR SYNAPSE (The Third Glutamate Reader) (The Slow-Burn Tuner Contact) (COG)

Textbooks name three ionotropic glutamate receptors and then talk about two of them.

The third, the kainate receptor, named for a seaweed toxin from Japan that activates it, refused for decades to show a clear synaptic current, and when it finally did - at the hippocampal mossy fibre synapse in 1997 - the current was strangely small and strangely slow.

That slowness turned out to be the point: the kainate system is glutamate deployed not as a command but as a tuner, integrating bursts on the postsynaptic side and adjusting release on the presynaptic side.

It is the demonstration, within a single messenger, that a receptor family defines a synapse type.

Slow-burn names the current: an ionotropic receptor whose synaptic response rises and decays roughly ten times slower than its alpha-amino-3-hydroxy-5-methyl-4-isoxazolepropionic acid (AMPA) cousin, summing across a burst like an ember rather than a spark.

Tuner names its principal job on both sides of the cleft: setting excitability and release probability rather than driving spikes outright.

Contact marks it as a classical chemical synapse in its pure-type form.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT TRANSMITS:

A glutamatergic synapse whose defining reader is the kainate receptor, an ionotropic cation channel built from the subunits GluK1 to GluK5, appearing postsynaptically as a slow excitatory current and presynaptically as an autoreceptor that adjusts glutamate release.

It transmits burst intensity: single spikes barely register, while trains are integrated into a sustained depolarisation and into a change of the terminal's own release probability.

THE JUNCTIONAL ARCHITECTURE:

The same asymmetric Gray type I build as Chapter 1 - bouton, cleft of about 20 to 24 nanometres, postsynaptic density - because the kainate receptor shares real estate with AMPA and N-methyl-D-aspartate (NMDA) receptors rather than owning a separate contact.

Its distinguishing placement is dual: clusters within and beside the postsynaptic density, and receptors on the presynaptic terminal membrane itself, where they taste the synapse's own spillover.

At the giant mossy fibre bouton of Part Three, the type's showcase address, one terminal engulfs a multi-headed thorny excrescence with dozens of release sites.

THE MOLECULAR MACHINERY:

Standard glutamate vesicle and SNARE machinery presynaptically, as in Chapter 1, SNARE being the soluble N-ethylmaleimide-sensitive factor attachment protein receptor family of fusion proteins.

The receptor itself is a tetramer of GluK1 to GluK3 subunits, with GluK4 and GluK5 as high-affinity partners that cannot form channels alone; the auxiliary proteins Neto1 and Neto2 slow the channel's kinetics into its synaptic signature, and the GluK2 plus GluK5 plus Neto1 combination is the mossy fibre receptor.

THE PARTNERS (THE LAYER 6 CAST):

The canonical pairing is dentate granule cell to CA3 pyramidal cell, the sparse-code teacher pathway of the hippocampal cast.

Kainate receptors also sit on cortical and hippocampal interneurons - basket and other GABAergic classes - where they both excite the interneuron and, as presynaptic receptors, modulate its gamma-aminobutyric acid release, and on dorsal root ganglion C-fibre terminals in the spinal cord.

SIZE AND SCALE:

At ordinary contacts the receptor complement is small, on the order of a few to a few tens of kainate receptors against tens of AMPA receptors, which is why the current component is about a tenth of the AMPA component.

At the mossy fibre bouton the presynaptic structure is giant, 3 to 8 micrometres across, though the kainate current remains a minority share of the total.

NUMBER AND DISTRIBUTION:

A minority system: kainate-receptor-dominated contacts number far below the Chapter 1 majority, concentrated in hippocampus, superficial neocortex, amygdala, and spinal cord, with GluK1 enriched on interneurons and sensory afferents and GluK2 on principal cells.

Its influence exceeds its count because it sits at high-leverage addresses, above all the mossy fibre gateway into the autoassociative network.

WHERE IT IS INSTALLED (THE LAYER 9 MAP):

• Hippocampus, CA3 stratum lucidum, where the mossy fibre synapse carries the type specimen postsynaptic kainate current.

• Hippocampal and neocortical interneuron networks, where kainate receptors tune inhibitory tone and gamma-band activity.

• Amygdala, where GluK1-containing receptors modulate fear circuits and are an anxiolytic drug target.

• Spinal cord dorsal horn, where kainate receptors on C-fibre terminals modulate pain transmission.

• Retina, where GluK1-containing receptors serve a class of off cone bipolar cell contacts.

THE TRANSFER FUNCTION:

Sign positive postsynaptically: a small cation conductance, single-channel conductance on the order of 1 to 20 picosiemens, contributing roughly 10 percent of the peak excitatory current at mossy fibre contacts but a much larger share of the charge because of its slow decay.

The character is integrating and supralinear across a burst: slow kinetics let successive spikes ride on the undecayed remainder of the last, so the current reports spike count and rate, not single events.

Presynaptically the function is biphasic: modest glutamate levels facilitate release through depolarising autoreceptors, while high levels depress it, a bell-shaped gain curve.

TEMPORAL SIGNATURE:

The synaptic kainate current rises over several milliseconds and decays with a time constant of roughly 10 to 100 milliseconds - about tenfold slower than AMPA at the same contact - the slowness conferred by GluK4 and GluK5 subunits with Neto auxiliaries rather than by the pore-forming subunits alone.

Meaningful integration therefore begins at burst frequencies above roughly 10 to 20 hertz, exactly the granule cell's burst regime.

SHORT-TERM DYNAMICS:

This synapse's home address shows the most dramatic facilitation in the brain: mossy fibre release probability starts very low, near 0.01 to 0.1, and trains facilitate transmission up to tenfold or more, with presynaptic kainate autoreceptors contributing a component of that growth over tens to hundreds of milliseconds.

Facilitation dominates because baseline release probability is low; heterosynaptic spillover from neighbouring pathways can also facilitate, as shown by its loss in GluK4 and GluK5 knockouts.

LONG-TERM PLASTICITY:

Mossy fibre long-term potentiation is presynaptic and NMDA-receptor-independent, expressed as a lasting rise in release probability through the cyclic adenosine monophosphate and protein kinase A pathway, with presynaptic kainate receptors implicated in its induction.

Postsynaptic kainate currents themselves also potentiate and depress, and the receptors' own trafficking is activity-dependent, so the type both learns and teaches.

RELIABILITY AND NOISE:

At single spikes the mossy fibre synapse fails most of the time, by design; across a burst the facilitated terminal becomes a conditional detonator that can fire CA3 almost deterministically.

The kainate component inherits this regime: negligible and noisy for lone spikes, reliable as a burst integrator.

ENERGY BUDGET:

Cheap per event because the currents are small, but the type pays a standing metabolic cost in the giant terminals it favours, whose vesicle cycling during facilitated bursts is intense; overall it is a small line item beside Chapter 1.

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY:

Kainate receptor expression peaks early: GluK1 in particular is high in the first postnatal weeks, when kainate signalling regulates axon growth, synapse formation, and network bursts, then falls to the adult minority level.

The mossy fibre pathway itself matures late and continues to be rebuilt lifelong by adult neurogenesis in the dentate gyrus.

EVOLUTIONARY PROVENANCE:

Kainate receptors are the closest ionotropic glutamate family to the ancestral invertebrate receptors, and the seaweed toxin that names them works because the sea and the synapse share chemistry; the vertebrate innovation is the high-affinity GluK4 and GluK5 subunits and the Neto proteins that slow the channel into a burst reader.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytic glutamate transporters set the spillover that the presynaptic and heterosynaptic kainate receptors read, making glial clearance an actual parameter of this synapse's gain rather than mere housekeeping.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS MICROCIRCUITS:

In the Layer 8 catalogue this junction flavours the expansion-then-detonation entry to the autoassociative memory motif: the kainate component helps convert the dentate's sparse burst code into reliable CA3 pattern induction.

In interneuron networks it is a gamma tuner, and without it those motifs would keep their wiring but lose their burst sensitivity - the difference between a circuit that counts spikes and one that merely notices them.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS:

Kainate injection is a standard animal model of temporal lobe epilepsy, and aberrant recurrent mossy fibre sprouting in human epilepsy creates kainate-receptor-rich contacts that sustain seizures.

GRIK2 mutations cause intellectual disability and neurodevelopmental disorder, and GRIK gene variants have been associated with mood disorders and with lithium response.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• SUBSCRIBES (INPUTS).

◦ Presynaptic burst trains, most effective above about 10 to 20 hertz.

◦ Ambient and spillover glutamate, micromolar range, read by presynaptic autoreceptors.

• PUBLISHES (OUTPUTS).

◦ A slow postsynaptic cation current, roughly 10 percent of peak excitatory current, decay 10 to 100 milliseconds.

◦ A presynaptic release-probability adjustment, facilitating at low glutamate and depressing at high.

• STATE.

◦ Integrated depolarisation across the burst; presynaptic residual calcium and release probability 0.01 to 0.5; receptor count as the slow weight.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Subunit mix GluK1 to GluK5 plus Neto1 or Neto2; baseline release probability near 0.02 at mossy fibres; kainate to AMPA current ratio about 0.1.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Current decay 10 to 100 milliseconds; facilitation tens to hundreds of milliseconds; presynaptic long-term potentiation minutes to hours.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Temporal lobe epilepsy with mossy fibre sprouting; GRIK2-linked intellectual disability; excitotoxic CA3 injury in kainate poisoning from contaminated shellfish (domoic acid).

THE ENGINEERING NOTE:

Propose the connection type burst_integrating_modulatory_connection.

A message on this connection carries a short spike train whose value is a saturating function of its count and rate, and the connection writes back a gain term onto its own source.

The nearest artificial intelligence analogue is a recurrent edge with a leaky integrator on the input, with the biological difference that the integrator also modulates the sender's future output - a weight that edits its own presynapse.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to integrate a burst of spikes through a slow kainate current that single spikes cannot exploit.
Create a number variable called kainate current with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called decay factor with initial value 0.9.
Create a number variable called increment per spike with initial value 0.2.
Create a number variable called firing threshold with initial value 0.5.
Create a list variable called spike train holding the burst as ones and zeros per millisecond.
For each time step in spike train, perform the following actions.
    Calculate the product of kainate current and decay factor.
    Set kainate current to the result.
    If the current time step holds a spike is true, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the sum of kainate current and increment per spike.
        Set kainate current to the result.
    End the conditional block.
    If kainate current is greater than firing threshold is true, then perform the following actions.
        Display the message burst detected to the console.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because a leaky integrator over a spike train is three lines of arithmetic, and the burst-versus-single contrast prints as one table.

# Slow kainate integration: decay ~10x slower than AMPA lets bursts
# summate where single spikes vanish (Castillo et al., Nature 1997;
# mossy fibre kainate EPSC decay tens of ms vs ~3 ms for AMPA).
import math

DT = 1.0                      # ms per step
TAU_AMPA, TAU_KA = 3.0, 40.0  # decay time constants, ms
AMP = 0.2                     # normalised increment per spike

def run(spike_times, tau, t_end=120):
    i, peak = 0.0, 0.0
    for t in range(int(t_end / DT)):
        i *= math.exp(-DT / tau)
        if t in spike_times:
            i += AMP
        peak = max(peak, i)
    return peak

single = {10}
burst = {10, 20, 30, 40, 50}          # five spikes at 100 Hz
for name, spikes in (("single spike", single), ("100 Hz burst", burst)):
    pa = run(spikes, TAU_AMPA)
    pk = run(spikes, TAU_KA)
    print(f"{name:13s}  AMPA peak {pa:4.2f}   kainate peak {pk:4.2f}   "
          f"burst-detected {pk > 0.5}")
# AMPA reports each spike alike; the kainate trace climbs across the
# burst and alone crosses threshold - an ionotropic rate reader.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

An ion channel wearing a metabotropic temperament: the only fast-transmitter synapse whose defining current is too slow to report a single spike, and whose receptors stand on both sides of the cleft, reading the synapse's own traffic to retune it.


CHAPTER 3. THE METABOTROPIC GLUTAMATE SYNAPSE (mGluR G-Protein-Coupled Contact) (The Meaning-of-More Contact) (COG)

The first two chapters read glutamate as electricity; this one reads it as news.

Around the edge of the excitatory synapse, just outside the receptor grid, stand receptors that open no channel at all: they hand the message to G-proteins, molecular switches inside the receiving cell, and the reply unfolds over seconds.

They fire mainly when transmission is heavy - when glutamate spills past the cleft's edge - so what they actually detect is not the message but its intensity: the meaning of more.

From that perch they set the gain of the synapse, gate its plasticity, and order the retrograde endocannabinoid reply of a later entry.

Meaning names the metabotropic mode: the transmitter is interpreted through an internal cascade rather than converted directly into current.

More names the trigger: perisynaptic placement makes these receptors spillover detectors, silent for single quanta and loud for bursts.

Contact marks the perisynaptic annulus as a genuine junctional territory, owned by the same two cells as the core synapse.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT TRANSMITS:

A signalling territory in which glutamate acts on metabotropic glutamate receptors (mGluRs), G-protein-coupled receptors of eight kinds, mGluR1 to mGluR8, sorted into three groups by their coupling.

It transmits a slow, graded report of synaptic intensity, whose consequences are changes of excitability, of release probability, and of plasticity thresholds rather than fast currents.

THE JUNCTIONAL ARCHITECTURE:

Not a separate cleft but an annulus: group I receptors (mGluR1 and mGluR5) form a perisynaptic ring on the postsynaptic membrane just outside the postsynaptic density, held by Homer scaffolds; group II (mGluR2 and mGluR3) and group III (mGluR4, mGluR6, mGluR7, mGluR8) sit mainly on the presynaptic terminal, group III inside the active zone itself.

The geometry is the computation: receptors placed at increasing distance from the release site read increasing thresholds of spillover.

THE MOLECULAR MACHINERY:

Each receptor is a dimer with a large clamshell glutamate-binding domain; group I couples through the G-protein Gq to phospholipase C, producing inositol trisphosphate, which releases calcium from internal stores, and diacylglycerol, which with calcium makes the endocannabinoid 2-arachidonoylglycerol.

Groups II and III couple through Gi and Go proteins, inhibiting adenylyl cyclase and, presynaptically, inhibiting voltage-gated calcium channels; the Homer family scaffolds group I receptors to the inositol trisphosphate receptor, wiring cleft to calcium store.

THE PARTNERS (THE LAYER 6 CAST):

Group I receptors decorate principal cells throughout the forebrain - mGluR5 on cortical, hippocampal, and striatal neurons, mGluR1 most famously on the cerebellar Purkinje cell, where it is the receptor of motor learning.

Group II and III receptors sit on the terminals of pyramidal cells, granule cells, and sensory afferents as brakes; astrocytes carry mGluR3, making the glial third party a direct subscriber; and mGluR6 belongs to the retinal on bipolar cell, where it inverts the photoreceptor's sign.

SIZE AND SCALE:

The perisynaptic annulus occupies roughly the 20 to 100 nanometre band around the postsynaptic density edge, with receptor numbers per synapse in the tens.

The receptor itself is large, a dimer of about 200 kilodaltons per subunit, with an extracellular domain roughly 15 nanometres tall - a molecular watchtower beside the cleft.

NUMBER AND DISTRIBUTION:

Present at most glutamatergic synapses in some group combination, hence numbered in the tens of trillions of installations, but always as an accessory system: mGluR5 densest in cortex, hippocampus, and striatum, mGluR1 in cerebellum and thalamus, mGluR2 marking perforant path and corticothalamic terminals, mGluR6 confined to the retina.

WHERE IT IS INSTALLED (THE LAYER 9 MAP):

• Cerebellar cortex, where mGluR1 at the parallel fibre to Purkinje synapse is required for long-term depression and motor learning.

• Hippocampus, where group I receptors gate long-term depression at CA3 to CA1 contacts and group II receptors govern the perforant path.

• Neocortex, where mGluR5 sets pyramidal excitability and drives endocannabinoid feedback.

• Striatum and basal ganglia, where mGluR5 partners dopamine receptors on spiny projection neurons and group III receptors brake the indirect pathway.

• Thalamus, where cortical layer 6 feedback excites relay cells through mGluR1, a slow attention signal.

• Retina, where mGluR6 on on bipolar cells performs the sign inversion that creates the on channel of vision.

• Hypothalamus and brainstem, where group II and III receptors restrain stress and autonomic drive.

THE TRANSFER FUNCTION:

Sign conditional: group I is net excitatory and sensitising, closing potassium channels and releasing store calcium; groups II and III are net inhibitory, reducing release probability by tens of percent.

The character is saturating and threshold-gated on input intensity - roughly a sigmoid on recent synaptic traffic - and the output is multiplicative: not a current added to the sum but a gain applied to the synapse's own transfer, with amplification of thousands of effector molecules per receptor binding.

TEMPORAL SIGNATURE:

Onset tens to hundreds of milliseconds after the triggering burst - G-protein cycling takes that long - with effects lasting seconds to minutes, and the slow excitatory postsynaptic potential of group I receptors rising over roughly 100 milliseconds and decaying over one to several seconds.

This is the slowest glutamate reader by two orders of magnitude, deliberately mismatched to single spikes.

SHORT-TERM DYNAMICS:

The system does not facilitate or depress in the vesicular sense; rather it is the controller of those dynamics, since presynaptic group II and III activation lowers release probability and thereby converts depressing contacts toward facilitating ones.

Its own responses desensitise over seconds of sustained agonist through receptor phosphorylation and arrestin binding.

LONG-TERM PLASTICITY:

This is the brain's principal long-term depression machine: group I activation induces mGluR-dependent long-term depression at hippocampal CA1 synapses through rapid local protein synthesis, and mGluR1 is obligatory for cerebellar long-term depression at the parallel fibre synapse.

Group I receptors also set the threshold for N-methyl-D-aspartate (NMDA)-receptor plasticity - metaplasticity, the learning rule about learning rules - so the type is tagged (COG) chiefly for governing the learning of Chapter 1.

RELIABILITY AND NOISE:

Single quanta rarely reach the annulus, so the system has an honest detection threshold rather than a failure rate; once triggered, second-messenger amplification makes the response robust, and store calcium release can be regenerative, an all-or-nothing internal wave.

ENERGY BUDGET:

Modest at the membrane but real inside the cell: guanosine triphosphate consumed by G-protein cycling, adenosine triphosphate spent refilling calcium stores and running kinase cascades, and the protein synthesis it commands is among the more expensive orders a synapse can give.

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY:

Group I signalling peaks in early postnatal life, where it drives synapse maturation and the critical-period pruning of climbing fibres in the cerebellum; the Homer scaffold assembles the perisynaptic ring as spines mature.

In the fragile X syndrome model, loss of the fragile X messenger ribonucleoprotein exaggerates mGluR5-dependent protein synthesis - the mGluR theory of fragile X - making this system a case study in developmental calibration.

EVOLUTIONARY PROVENANCE:

The clamshell glutamate-binding domain descends from bacterial amino-acid-binding proteins, and metabotropic glutamate reading likely predates the ionotropic synapse's dominance; the vertebrate radiation into eight receptors and three groups is a diversification of placement and coupling, not of chemistry.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocyte transporters define the spillover boundary that gives the annulus its threshold, and astrocytic mGluR3 lets the glial partner eavesdrop on the same signal, closing the tripartite loop of Part Seven.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS MICROCIRCUITS:

In the Layer 8 catalogue this system is the gain and threshold fabric: the cerebellar perceptron's teacher-gated depression, the attractor network's metaplastic stability, and the thalamic attention gate's slow cortical drive all run on mGluR signalling.

Without it the microcircuits would keep transmitting but lose their tuning - attractors would saturate, cerebellar learning would stop, and the cortex would lose its volume knob on its own excitement.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS:

Fragile X syndrome, the most common inherited intellectual disability, is in significant part a disorder of exaggerated mGluR5-dependent plasticity.

Autoantibodies against mGluR1 cause a paraneoplastic cerebellar ataxia, GRM1 mutations cause spinocerebellar ataxia, mGluR5 signalling is implicated in addiction relapse and levodopa-induced dyskinesia, and group II agonism was a major, ultimately unsuccessful, schizophrenia drug programme.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• SUBSCRIBES (INPUTS).

◦ Spillover glutamate from burst transmission, effective above roughly 10 hertz sustained input.

◦ Homer-scaffold state and store calcium load.

• PUBLISHES (OUTPUTS).

◦ Postsynaptic gain change: potassium channel closure, store calcium release, slow depolarisation of 1 to 10 millivolts.

◦ Presynaptic release-probability reduction of roughly 20 to 50 percent (groups II and III).

◦ Endocannabinoid synthesis command to the retrograde channel of entry 15.

◦ Long-term depression induction and NMDA-plasticity threshold setting.

• STATE.

◦ Active G-protein fraction; inositol trisphosphate and store calcium levels; local protein synthesis rate as the plasticity variable.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Group and subtype mix per synapse; annulus radius 20 to 100 nanometres; amplification of order 10 to the power 3 effectors per binding.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Onset 50 to several hundred milliseconds; slow potential decay 1 to 10 seconds; desensitisation seconds; long-term depression tens of minutes to lasting.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Fragile X syndrome (FMR1 silencing, runaway mGluR5-dependent depression); anti-mGluR1 cerebellar ataxia; GRM1 spinocerebellar ataxia; mGluR5-linked addiction and dyskinesia circuits.

THE ENGINEERING NOTE:

Propose the connection type slow_gain_setting_connection.

A message on this connection carries no payload of its own; it carries a multiplier and a learning-rate adjustment addressed to another connection, typically the fast excitatory edge it surrounds.

The nearest artificial intelligence analogue is a hypernetwork or a learned learning-rate schedule - parameters that set other parameters - with the biological difference that the controller is wrapped physically around the edge it controls and is driven by that edge's own overflow.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to set the gain and the learning rate of a fast synapse from the amount of spillover glutamate that reaches the perisynaptic ring.
Create a number variable called spillover with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called spillover threshold with initial value 0.6.
Create a number variable called synaptic gain with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called learning rate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called recent traffic holding the average synaptic activity over the last second.
Set spillover to recent traffic.
If spillover is greater than spillover threshold is true, then perform the following actions.
    Calculate the product of synaptic gain and 0.7.
    Set synaptic gain to the result.
    Set learning rate to 0.5.
Otherwise, perform the following actions.
    Set synaptic gain to 1.
    Set learning rate to 0.1.
End the conditional block.
Display synaptic gain to the console.
Display learning rate to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because a controller that rescales another synapse's parameters is clearest as a small class acting on a number it does not own.

# Metabotropic gain setting: perisynaptic mGluRs read spillover
# (a running average of traffic, not single quanta) and reply by
# rescaling the fast synapse's gain and plasticity threshold
# (Luscher & Huber, Neuron 2010, group I mGluR-LTD).
class MetabotropicController:
    def __init__(self, threshold=0.6, tau=1.0):
        self.threshold, self.tau = threshold, tau
        self.traffic = 0.0            # slow running average, ~1 s

    def step(self, activity, dt=0.05):
        self.traffic += (activity - self.traffic) * dt / self.tau
        heavy = self.traffic > self.threshold
        gain = 0.7 if heavy else 1.0          # presynaptic damping
        learn_rate = 0.5 if heavy else 0.1    # LTD gate opens
        return gain, learn_rate

ctrl = MetabotropicController()
print("t(s)  input  traffic  gain  learn")
for t in range(40):
    activity = 1.0 if 10 <= t < 30 else 0.1   # a 1-second heavy burst
    gain, lr = ctrl.step(activity)
    if t % 5 == 0:
        print(f"{t*0.05:4.1f}  {activity:5.1f}  {ctrl.traffic:7.2f}"
              f"  {gain:4.1f}  {lr:5.1f}")
# The controller ignores brief inputs, then turns the synapse's own
# volume down and its learning up only under sustained load.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The synapse that never carries the message: a ring of watchtower receptors around the cleft's edge whose only input is another synapse's overflow, and whose only outputs are that synapse's gain and its permission to learn.


CHAPTER 4. THE GABA-A FAST INHIBITORY SYNAPSE (Ionotropic GABA Receptor Synapse) (The Millisecond Brake Contact) (COG)

For every four or five synapses that say go, one says stop, and this is the one.

Gamma-aminobutyric acid, made from glutamate by the removal of one chemical group, opens a chloride channel within a millisecond of release, and the resulting current does not so much push the neuron down as hold it still.

Every interneuron motif in the Layer 8 catalogue - basket, chandelier, Martinotti, neurogliaform - delivers its authority through this single junction type, differing only in where it lands and how fast it decays.

It is also the most medicated synapse in the human pharmacopoeia: benzodiazepines, barbiturates, most general anaesthetics, and alcohol all act here.

Millisecond names its speed: the fastest inhibitory current in the forebrain, opening in under a millisecond and decaying in a few to tens.

Brake names its job, with the honest note that the brake works two ways: hyperpolarising the membrane, and shunting it, which is braking by short circuit.

Contact marks it as a classical chemical synapse, the Gray type II symmetric build.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT TRANSMITS:

A chemical synapse releasing gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA), the brain's principal inhibitory transmitter, onto GABA-A receptors, which are ionotropic chloride channels.

It transmits a fast conductance whose effect depends on the chloride battery of the receiving cell: below the chloride reversal potential it hyperpolarises, near it it shunts, and in immature or injured neurons with high internal chloride it can even excite.

THE JUNCTIONAL ARCHITECTURE:

The Gray type II symmetric synapse: presynaptic bouton with small flattened-appearing vesicles, a slightly narrower cleft of about 12 to 20 nanometres, and a thin postsynaptic density built on the scaffold gephyrin rather than PSD-95.

Its placements are the geometry chapters of Part Two in advance: dendritic shaft, cell body, and axon initial segment, almost never a spine head of its own.

THE MOLECULAR MACHINERY:

GABA is synthesised from glutamate by glutamic acid decarboxylase (GAD65 and GAD67) and loaded by the vesicular inhibitory amino acid transporter VIAAT; release uses the same SNARE machinery as Chapter 1, SNARE being the soluble N-ethylmaleimide-sensitive factor attachment protein receptor fusion complex.

The receptor is a pentamer, most commonly two alpha, two beta, and one gamma subunit drawn from nineteen genes; alpha1-containing receptors decay fast and mark parvalbumin basket synapses, alpha2 marks initial segment and somatic synapses with slower decay, alpha5 and delta-containing receptors serve slow and extrasynaptic inhibition, and the gamma2 subunit plus gephyrin and collybistin anchor the cluster.

The benzodiazepine site sits at the alpha-gamma interface - a drug target built from the seam between two subunits.

THE PARTNERS (THE LAYER 6 CAST):

Presynaptically, the entire inhibitory nobility of the Layer 6 catalogue: parvalbumin basket and chandelier cells, somatostatin Martinotti cells, vasoactive intestinal peptide disinhibitors, neurogliaform cells, striatal spiny projection neurons, cerebellar Purkinje cells, and thalamic reticular neurons.

Postsynaptically, every neuron class in the forebrain: no cell type in the catalogue is without this brake.

SIZE AND SCALE:

Boutons of about 0.5 to 1.5 micrometres; postsynaptic clusters hold on the order of 30 to 200 receptors; quantal conductance is roughly 0.3 to 1 nanosiemens with single-channel conductance about 27 to 30 picosiemens.

A basket cell places 5 to 15 boutons on one target soma, and a chandelier cartridge strings 3 to 10 along one initial segment.

NUMBER AND DISTRIBUTION:

Roughly 10 to 20 percent of all synapses in cortex - on the order of 10 to the power 13 contacts brain-wide - but concentrated where they count: the perisomatic region of a pyramidal cell is almost exclusively GABAergic.

Present in every region of the central nervous system, with the cerebellar and striatal output stages being almost entirely this type.

WHERE IT IS INSTALLED (THE LAYER 9 MAP):

• Neocortex, where perisomatic GABA-A synapses pace gamma rhythms and enforce the balance of excitation and inhibition.

• Hippocampus, where interneuron classes tile the pyramidal cell surface and sculpt theta and ripple timing.

• Striatum and globus pallidus, where the entire basal ganglia selection chain is GABA-A inhibition in series.

• Cerebellum, where Purkinje cells deliver the cortex's only output as GABA onto the deep nuclei.

• Thalamus, where reticular nucleus synapses generate sleep spindles and gate relay mode.

• Brainstem and spinal cord, where GABA-A works beside glycine in sensory gating and motor control.

• Retina, where horizontal and amacrine cell circuits use it for lateral and temporal shaping.

THE TRANSFER FUNCTION:

Sign negative in the adult: a chloride conductance of 0.3 to 1 nanosiemens per quantum with reversal near minus 70 millivolts, close to rest.

The character is therefore divisive as much as subtractive: with little driving force the synapse adds conductance rather than current, dividing the neuron's response to simultaneous excitation - shunting inhibition - while at depolarised potentials the same synapse pulls genuinely down.

Placement completes the arithmetic: somatic and initial segment contacts divide the output, dendritic contacts divide a single branch's input.

TEMPORAL SIGNATURE:

Rise time under 1 millisecond; decay time constant about 3 to 10 milliseconds for alpha1-containing synapses and 10 to 30 milliseconds or more for alpha2- and alpha3-containing ones, the subunit mix acting as a per-synapse clock setting.

A separate slow mode, GABA-A slow, decays over about 50 milliseconds at neurogliaform contacts, and extrasynaptic alpha5 and delta receptors sustain a standing tonic conductance with no time signature at all.

SHORT-TERM DYNAMICS:

Depression dominates: release probability at basket synapses is high, around 0.5 to 0.9, so trains depress by tens of percent with recovery over hundreds of milliseconds - a design choice favouring reliable first strikes over sustained argument.

Some interneuron classes facilitate, and retrograde endocannabinoids (entry 15) transiently suppress this synapse from cholecystokinin baskets, the phenomenon of depolarisation-induced suppression of inhibition.

LONG-TERM PLASTICITY:

Real but quieter than Chapter 1: inhibitory long-term potentiation and depression exist, expressed through gephyrin scaffold remodelling and receptor number, and chloride regulation itself is plastic, shifting the reversal potential.

Much of this plasticity is homeostatic - inhibition learns to match local excitation - so the synapse is tagged (COG) as the adjustable governor of learning networks rather than as the memory store itself.

RELIABILITY AND NOISE:

Among the more reliable cortical synapses: multi-bouton innervation and high release probability give a basket-to-pyramid connection a failure rate near zero and coefficient of variation far below single excitatory contacts.

The brake, unlike the accelerator, is engineered not to miss.

ENERGY BUDGET:

Cheaper per event than excitation because chloride sits near its equilibrium - little current flows at rest - but the interneurons that use it fire at high rates, so the parvalbumin network's fast-spiking metabolism, not the synapse itself, is the real bill.

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY:

The famous developmental reversal: immature neurons keep internal chloride high through the transporter NKCC1, so GABA-A currents depolarise and help wire the network, then the transporter KCC2 takes over, chloride falls, and the same synapse becomes the adult brake.

Gephyrin clusters assemble opposite GABAergic terminals guided by neuroligin-2, and the parvalbumin perisomatic system matures late, closing cortical critical periods as it does.

EVOLUTIONARY PROVENANCE:

The pentameric receptor family descends from the same ancestral ligand-gated channel as the nicotinic receptor - the brake and the neuromuscular accelerator are cousins - and GABA signalling itself is older than nervous systems, present in bacteria and plants.

The nineteen-subunit expansion is the vertebrate refinement, allowing per-synapse kinetic and pharmacological tuning.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes take up GABA through the transporter GAT-3 and can release it back tonically; presynaptic terminals recapture it through GAT-1, the target of the antiepileptic tiagabine.

Perineuronal nets, the extracellular lattices of the other-cells catalogue, ensheath the parvalbumin cells that own most of these synapses, stabilising their wiring.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS MICROCIRCUITS:

This junction is the fabric of the entire inhibitory division of the Layer 8 catalogue: feedforward and feedback inhibition, lateral competition, gamma pacing, sleep spindle generation, and the basal ganglia's inhibition-in-series selection all are this synapse in different placements.

Without it the microcircuit layer would not degrade but explode: excitation without this brake is a seizure, within seconds.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS:

Epilepsy is this synapse's failure in a dozen forms, including GABRA1, GABRB3, and GABRG2 subunit mutations and Dravet-syndrome interneuron failure; benzodiazepines treat seizures and anxiety by enhancing it, and their withdrawal is its rebound.

Anxiety disorders, schizophrenia's parvalbumin and GAD67 deficits, stiff person syndrome's anti-GAD autoimmunity, and the sedation of alcohol and general anaesthetics are all chapters of this synapse's pathology and pharmacology.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• SUBSCRIBES (INPUTS).

◦ Interneuron spike trains, up to several hundred hertz from fast-spiking classes.

◦ The target cell's chloride gradient, set by KCC2 and NKCC1.

◦ Modulators: benzodiazepine-site ligands, neurosteroids, endocannabinoid suppression of release.

• PUBLISHES (OUTPUTS).

◦ A chloride conductance of 0.3 to 1 nanosiemens per quantum, reversal about minus 70 millivolts.

◦ Hyperpolarising current when the membrane is depolarised; pure shunt near rest.

• STATE.

◦ The weight: postsynaptic receptor count 30 to 200 and gephyrin cluster size; presynaptic release probability; the chloride reversal potential as a slow state variable.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Subunit composition (alpha1, alpha2, alpha3, alpha5, gamma2, delta); placement class (shaft, soma, initial segment); boutons per connection 3 to 15.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Rise under 1 millisecond; decay 3 to 30 milliseconds by subunit; GABA-A slow about 50 milliseconds; depression recovery hundreds of milliseconds; chloride regulation minutes to days.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Genetic epilepsies (GABRA1, GABRB3, GABRG2, suprachiasmatic (SCN)1A interneuron failure); stiff person syndrome (anti-GAD65); benzodiazepine dependence and withdrawal seizures; depolarising GABA after injury and in chronic pain.

THE ENGINEERING NOTE:

Propose the connection type fast_shunting_inhibitory_connection.

A message on this connection carries a spike timestamp and applies both a negative bias and, crucially, a divisive gain term at a specified compartment of the destination.

The nearest artificial intelligence analogue is a combination of a negative-weighted edge and a normalisation operation, with the biological difference that the division is implemented by physics - added conductance in a leaky membrane - and is addressed to a location, not just a neuron.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to show that the same inhibitory synapse divides the response near the chloride reversal potential and subtracts from it when the membrane is depolarised.
Create a number variable called membrane voltage with initial value minus 65.
Create a number variable called chloride reversal with initial value minus 70.
Create a number variable called excitatory current with initial value 2.
Create a number variable called leak conductance with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called inhibitory conductance with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called response with initial value 0.
Calculate the quotient of excitatory current and leak conductance.
Set response to the result.
Display the message response without inhibition to the console, followed by response.
Set inhibitory conductance to 3.
Calculate the sum of leak conductance and inhibitory conductance.
Calculate the quotient of excitatory current and the result.
Set response to the result.
Display the message response with shunting inhibition to the console, followed by response.
If membrane voltage is greater than chloride reversal is true, then perform the following actions.
    Calculate the difference of membrane voltage and chloride reversal.
    Calculate the product of the result and inhibitory conductance.
    Display the message additional hyperpolarising current to the console, followed by the result.
End the conditional block.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the shunting-versus-hyperpolarising distinction is one steady-state equation evaluated at two voltages, and a table makes the arithmetic visible.

# Shunting vs hyperpolarising inhibition at a GABA-A synapse.
# Near E_Cl the synapse adds conductance, not current: it DIVIDES the
# response to excitation. Depolarised, the same synapse also SUBTRACTS.
# (Koch, Biophysics of Computation 1999; E_Cl ~ -70 mV in adult cortex.)
E_L, E_CL, E_EX = -65.0, -70.0, 0.0     # mV: leak, chloride, excitatory
G_L = 10.0                              # nS leak conductance

def steady_voltage(g_ex, g_in):
    # Conductance-weighted average of the batteries.
    num = G_L * E_L + g_ex * E_EX + g_in * E_CL
    return num / (G_L + g_ex + g_in)

print(" g_in(nS)  V rest(mV)  depol. by 2 nS excitation (mV)")
for g_in in (0.0, 5.0, 20.0):
    v0 = steady_voltage(0.0, g_in)
    v1 = steady_voltage(2.0, g_in)
    print(f"{g_in:8.1f}  {v0:10.2f}  {v1 - v0:12.2f}")
# Rows: rest barely moves (E_Cl is near rest: little subtraction),
# but the depolarisation caused by the same excitation shrinks as g_in
# grows - inhibition as division, a gain control rather than a push.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The symmetric synapse whose battery sits at rest: the one junction that can veto a neuron while barely moving its voltage, delivered by every interneuron in the catalogue and answered by every neuron in the brain.


CHAPTER 5. THE GABA-B SLOW METABOTROPIC SYNAPSE (Metabotropic GABA Receptor Junction) (The Long-Hold Damper Contact) (COG)

Chapter 4's brake is a tap on the pedal; this chapter is the handbrake left on.

The same messenger, gamma-aminobutyric acid, read through a G-protein-coupled receptor instead of a channel, produces inhibition that begins only after tens of milliseconds and then holds for hundreds - not a veto of one spike but a change in what kind of activity is possible at all.

Postsynaptically it opens potassium channels that sag the whole neighbourhood of membrane; presynaptically it throttles calcium channels and turns transmitters down at the source.

It is the receptor of the neurogliaform cell's inhibitory blanket, the second half of every thalamic spindle cycle, and the reason baclofen relaxes a spastic muscle from inside the spinal cord.

Long-hold names the time signature: onset around 50 milliseconds, peak near 150 to 250, decay lasting half a second or more - inhibition as a state, not an event.

Damper names the mechanism: a potassium leak and a calcium throttle that lower the gain of everything beneath them, rather than a chloride shunt aimed at one compartment.

Contact is widened to junction in the formal descriptor because much of this signalling is volume transmission: receptors sit largely outside the cleft and answer spilled or blanket-released GABA.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT TRANSMITS:

A signalling mode in which gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) acts on the GABA-B receptor, an obligate dimer of two seven-transmembrane subunits, GABA-B1 binding the transmitter and GABA-B2 working the G-protein.

It transmits a slow inhibitory state change: a potassium conductance postsynaptically, a calcium-channel suppression presynaptically, and, over minutes, an adjustment of the cell's excitability set point.

THE JUNCTIONAL ARCHITECTURE:

There is no dedicated cleft: GABA-B receptors sit mainly perisynaptically and extrasynaptically on dendrites and on axon terminals, positioned to read GABA that escapes the Chapter 4 cleft during heavy interneuron firing.

The neurogliaform cell provides the type's one purpose-built presynapse: boutons that release into the neuropil at large, a cloud rather than an address, with the receptor's effector - the G-protein-gated inwardly rectifying potassium channel - clustered on dendritic shafts and spines nearby.

THE MOLECULAR MACHINERY:

The GABA-B1 and GABA-B2 subunits form the only known obligate-dimer neurotransmitter receptor; sushi-domain variants of GABA-B1a target it to axon terminals, and the KCTD auxiliary proteins (KCTD8, 12, 16) set the kinetics and desensitisation of the response.

The G-protein is Gi/Go: its beta-gamma arm opens G-protein-gated inwardly rectifying potassium (GIRK, also called Kir3) channels and inhibits P/Q-type and N-type voltage-gated calcium channels, while its alpha arm inhibits adenylyl cyclase, the enzyme that makes cyclic adenosine monophosphate.

THE PARTNERS (THE LAYER 6 CAST):

The signature presynaptic partner is the neurogliaform cell of the Layer 6 catalogue, the only interneuron whose single spike reliably evokes a GABA-B response; dense-firing parvalbumin and thalamic reticular populations recruit it through spillover.

Postsynaptically, pyramidal cells, thalamic relay cells, and interneurons all carry the receptor, and as a presynaptic heteroreceptor it sits on glutamatergic and monoaminergic terminals alike, making it a brake on transmitters it does not share a cell with.

SIZE AND SCALE:

Receptor densities are highest on distal dendrites and spine annuli, at tens to hundreds of receptors per micrometre squared in hotspots; the neurogliaform axon cloud spans a few hundred micrometres and can touch thousands of dendrites at once.

Unitary GABA-B conductances are small, of the order of 0.1 to 1 nanosiemens of potassium conductance per activated patch, but they are applied across wide stretches of membrane.

NUMBER AND DISTRIBUTION:

Expressed by most neurons of the central nervous system, with strong installations in neocortex, hippocampus, thalamus, cerebellum (the molecular layer is among the densest), spinal cord, and on peripheral autonomic and sensory terminals - a near-universal accessory rather than a counted contact type.

WHERE IT IS INSTALLED (THE LAYER 9 MAP):

• Neocortex, where layer 1 neurogliaform blankets damp the apical tufts that Chapter 1 of the Layer 6 volume made famous.

• Hippocampus, where GABA-B currents terminate place-cell bursts and pace theta-nested activity.

• Thalamus, where reticular GABA-B responses hyperpolarise relay cells into burst mode and set the slow rhythm of spindles and of absence seizures.

• Cerebellar cortex, where presynaptic GABA-B tunes the parallel fibre synapse of Part Three.

• Spinal cord dorsal horn, where presynaptic GABA-B on afferent terminals is the analgesic and antispastic site of baclofen.

• Brainstem monoamine nuclei, where GABA-B autoregulation shapes the broadcast systems of later chapters.

THE TRANSFER FUNCTION:

Sign negative, twice: postsynaptically a potassium conductance pulling toward about minus 90 millivolts - genuine hyperpolarisation, unlike Chapter 4's near-rest chloride - and presynaptically a reduction of calcium influx that cuts release probability by up to half or more.

The character is saturating and threshold-gated on input intensity: single interneuron spikes usually do nothing, sustained or synchronous firing recruits it steeply, and the G-protein cascade then amplifies each binding into many channel openings.

TEMPORAL SIGNATURE:

Onset latency about 20 to 50 milliseconds - the G-protein's paperwork - peak at roughly 100 to 250 milliseconds, and decay over 200 milliseconds to a second or more, with KCTD12 accelerating desensitisation in cells that need a shorter hold.

This is the slow half of the cortical inhibitory postsynaptic potential: a fast Chapter 4 trough, then this long shallow one.

SHORT-TERM DYNAMICS:

The response itself desensitises over seconds under sustained agonist, and its recruitment effectively facilitates with presynaptic burst length, since only accumulated spillover reaches the extrasynaptic receptors.

As a presynaptic controller it is itself a short-term dynamic imposed on other synapses, converting their depression toward facilitation by lowering release probability.

LONG-TERM PLASTICITY:

Modest and mostly regulatory: the receptor's surface number and its KCTD partners are activity-dependent, and by hyperpolarising dendrites it gates the N-methyl-D-aspartate (NMDA)-dependent plasticity of Chapter 1 - a permission system more than a memory store.

The honest statement is that this junction stores little itself; it decides when and where others may store.

RELIABILITY AND NOISE:

Recruitment is unreliable by design at low activity - a threshold system - but the delivered state change is smooth and low-noise, since the G-protein cascade averages over many receptors and channels.

The neurogliaform blanket is the exception: one spike, one reliable slow inhibitory potential across a whole neighbourhood.

ENERGY BUDGET:

Inexpensive per event - potassium flows downhill and little pumping is needed to restore it - and the cascade economises further by amplification; the system's real cost is opportunity, the transmission it prevents.

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY:

GABA-B function appears early - in the neonatal brain it is the principal true hyperpolarising inhibition while chloride is still high - and presynaptic GABA-B restrains transmitter release throughout the wiring period.

Receptor dimers assemble in the endoplasmic reticulum, GABA-B2 chaperoning GABA-B1 to the surface, a quality-control marriage with no divorce.

EVOLUTIONARY PROVENANCE:

The receptor belongs to class C of the G-protein-coupled receptor superfamily, sharing the clamshell binding domain and dimer habit of Chapter 3's metabotropic glutamate receptors - the slow readers of the two great transmitters are siblings, both descended from ancient nutrient-sensing proteins.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytic and neuronal GABA transporters set the spillover field on which extrasynaptic recruitment depends, and astrocytes themselves express GABA-B receptors, answering interneuron activity with calcium signals - the glial partner is a subscriber here, not just a janitor.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS MICROCIRCUITS:

In the Layer 8 catalogue this junction is the fabric of the slow oscillation and spindle motifs of the thalamocortical loop, of the layer 1 blanket that normalises whole dendritic neighbourhoods, and of every motif that needs an activity ceiling with a memory of the recent past.

Without it circuits would keep their millisecond manners but lose their paragraph structure: bursts would not terminate, spindles would not cycle, and cortical up-states would run on unpunctuated.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS:

Absence epilepsy is in part a GABA-B rhythm gone rigid in the thalamocortical loop, and GABBR1 and GABBR2 variants cause developmental epileptic encephalopathies.

Anti-GABA-B-receptor encephalitis presents with seizures and memory loss; baclofen treats spasticity through spinal GABA-B and its abrupt intrathecal withdrawal is a medical emergency; gamma-hydroxybutyrate intoxication acts substantially through this receptor.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• SUBSCRIBES (INPUTS).

◦ Spillover GABA from sustained or synchronous interneuron firing; single neurogliaform spikes.

◦ Baclofen and gamma-hydroxybutyrate as exogenous agonists.

• PUBLISHES (OUTPUTS).

◦ Postsynaptic potassium conductance toward minus 90 millivolts, unitary 0.1 to 1 nanosiemens, applied dendrite-wide.

◦ Presynaptic release-probability cuts of up to 50 percent or more on GABAergic, glutamatergic, and monoaminergic terminals.

◦ Cyclic adenosine monophosphate reduction as a minutes-scale excitability adjustment.

• STATE.

◦ Active G-protein and GIRK-open fractions; desensitisation level set by KCTD proteins; surface receptor number as the slow weight.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Dimer plus KCTD composition; receptor placement (dendritic, terminal, extrasynaptic); recruitment threshold in spikes per burst.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Onset 20 to 50 milliseconds; peak 100 to 250 milliseconds; decay 200 milliseconds to over 1 second; desensitisation seconds; receptor trafficking hours.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Absence epilepsy in the thalamocortical loop; GABBR1 and GABBR2 encephalopathies; anti-GABA-B-receptor limbic encephalitis; baclofen withdrawal syndrome; gamma-hydroxybutyrate overdose.

THE ENGINEERING NOTE:

Propose the connection type slow_state_inhibitory_connection.

A message on this connection carries a decaying inhibitory state - a scalar that raises the destination's threshold and lowers regional gain for hundreds of milliseconds - rather than an event.

The nearest artificial intelligence analogue is a slow negative bias with exponential decay, akin to an adaptive temperature or an activity regulariser, with the biological difference that it is delivered anatomically as a cloud, addressed to neighbourhoods rather than to units.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to impose a slow potassium-mediated inhibitory state that outlasts the burst which caused it and raises the firing threshold while it lasts.
Create a number variable called inhibitory state with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called state decay factor with initial value 0.99.
Create a number variable called recruitment threshold with initial value 3.
Create a number variable called spikes in recent burst with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called firing threshold with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called base threshold with initial value 1.
For each time step in the simulation, perform the following actions.
    Calculate the product of inhibitory state and state decay factor.
    Set inhibitory state to the result.
    Set spikes in recent burst to the count of interneuron spikes in the last fifty milliseconds.
    If spikes in recent burst is greater than recruitment threshold is true, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the sum of inhibitory state and 1.
        Set inhibitory state to the result.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the product of inhibitory state and 0.5.
    Calculate the sum of base threshold and the result.
    Set firing threshold to the result.
End the loop.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because a threshold-gated leaky state variable and its effect on excitability fit in one loop whose printout tells the whole story.

# Slow GABA-B state inhibition: recruited only by bursts (spillover
# threshold), rising over ~150 ms, decaying over ~500 ms via GIRK/Kir3
# potassium channels (Luscher & Slesinger, Nat Rev Neurosci 2010).
import math

DT = 10.0                      # ms per step
TAU_RISE, TAU_DECAY = 80.0, 500.0
THRESH_SPIKES = 3              # spikes/50 ms needed to recruit

drive, state = 0.0, 0.0        # G-protein drive and GIRK conductance
print("t(ms)  burst  gGIRK  V_shift(mV)  excitable")
for step in range(80):
    t = step * DT
    burst = 5 if 100 <= t < 150 else 0        # one strong burst
    recruit = 1.0 if burst > THRESH_SPIKES else 0.0
    drive += (recruit - drive) * DT / TAU_RISE
    state += (drive - state) * DT / TAU_DECAY
    v_shift = -8.0 * state                     # toward E_K, ~-90 mV
    if step % 8 == 0:
        print(f"{t:5.0f}  {burst:5d}  {state:5.2f}  {v_shift:10.2f}"
              f"  {v_shift > -2.0}")
# The hyperpolarisation peaks well AFTER the burst ends and holds the
# cell below its excitable range for ~half a second: inhibition as a
# state the network must wait out, not an event it can dodge.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The inhibition with a memory: the same molecule as the millisecond brake, but read by a dimer through a G-protein onto potassium, arriving late, staying long, delivered as a cloud, and felt not as a blow but as a change in the weather of the membrane.


END OF PART ONE, CHAPTERS 1 TO 5


CHAPTER 6. THE GLYCINERGIC SYNAPSE (The Fast Chloride Brake of the Cord) (NON-COG)

Below the neck the spinal cord runs its own reflex traffic without asking the cortex, and it needs a brake fast enough to stop a limb before a thought could form.

Glycine is that brake: the smallest amino acid, poured into a millisecond-wide chloride channel, holding the timing of every step and every breath.

Fast names its speed - inhibition delivered in about a millisecond, faster than any slow modulator.

Chloride names its currency - a negative ion flooding inward to clamp the cell quiet.

Brake names its office - it does not compute, it stops.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT TRANSMITS:

The glycinergic synapse is a fast inhibitory chemical synapse that releases the amino acid glycine to open a chloride-selective ion channel on the receiving cell, driving that cell away from its firing threshold.

Chloride is the negatively charged form of the element chlorine, and letting it into a cell makes the inside more negative, which is inhibition.

It is the dominant fast inhibitory synapse of the spinal cord and brainstem, the lower-body counterpart to the GABA-A synapse (gamma-aminobutyric acid type A) that dominates the forebrain.

THE JUNCTIONAL ARCHITECTURE:

A conventional small chemical synapse: a presynaptic terminal owned by an inhibitory interneuron, a synaptic cleft about 20 nanometres across (a nanometre being a millionth of a millimetre), and a postsynaptic membrane owned by the target neuron.

The presynaptic side owns the vesicles and release machinery; the postsynaptic side owns the receptor field and its anchoring scaffold.

Gray type II is the classical descriptor - a symmetric, flat-vesicle inhibitory contact, usually onto the dendritic shaft or cell body rather than a spine.

THE MOLECULAR MACHINERY:

Glycine is loaded into small clear synaptic vesicles by the vesicular inhibitory amino acid transporter, the same transporter that loads GABA, so many terminals release both.

Release uses the standard SNARE proteins (soluble N-ethylmaleimide-sensitive factor attachment protein receptors, the fusion proteins syntaxin, SNAP-25 and synaptobrevin) triggered by the calcium sensor synaptotagmin.

The receptor is the glycine receptor, a pentamer - five subunits around a central chloride pore - built in the adult from alpha-1 and beta subunits, with the beta subunit gripping the anchoring scaffold protein gephyrin that clusters receptors opposite the release site.

Glycine binds with a half-maximal concentration near 100 micromolar, and the channel is anion-selective, passing chloride.

THE PARTNERS (THE LAYER 6 CAST):

On the presynaptic side stand the cord's small inhibitory interneurons: pre-eminently the Renshaw cell, which takes a copy of a motor neuron's command and feeds inhibition straight back, and the Ia inhibitory interneuron of the reciprocal circuit that silences the antagonist muscle's motor pool.

On the postsynaptic side stand alpha motor neurons and other interneurons of the ventral cord, and second-order sensory and auditory brainstem neurons.

The partner personalities are built for reflex economy - fast, local, and stereotyped.

SIZE AND SCALE:

A small bouton, roughly 1 to 2 micrometres across, holding a few hundred vesicles.

The postsynaptic receptor cluster spans a few tenths of a micrometre and holds on the order of tens to low hundreds of channels.

NUMBER AND DISTRIBUTION:

Glycinergic synapses number in the many billions across the cord and brainstem but are scarce above the midbrain.

They concentrate in the ventral (motor) horn, the intermediate grey, and auditory brainstem nuclei.

WHERE IT IS INSTALLED (THE LAYER 9 MAP):

• The ventral horn of the spinal cord, where Renshaw feedback and reciprocal inhibition shape every motor command.

• The intermediate grey of the cord, where reflex arcs are gated and coordinated.

• The auditory brainstem, including the medial nucleus of the trapezoid body, where fast inhibition sharpens sound-localisation timing.

• The retina, where glycinergic amacrine cells shape the vertical signal flow.

• The brainstem respiratory network, where reciprocal inhibition times inspiration against expiration.

THE TRANSFER FUNCTION:
