• SUBSCRIBES (INPUTS).

◦ Acetylcholine bursts from Pitx2 cholinergic interneurons during effortful or locomotor tasks.

• PUBLISHES (OUTPUTS).

◦ A slow, multiplicative increase in motoneuron firing gain via reduced potassium conductance.

• STATE.

◦ M2 receptor activation level; Kv2.1 and SK conductance state; subsurface cistern calcium; motoneuron excitability set-point. No learned weight.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Terminal size 1 to 8 micrometres; cleft about 20 nanometres; postsynaptic M2 receptors and Kv2.1/SK channels over a subsurface cistern; dozens per motoneuron soma.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Metabotropic onset hundreds of milliseconds to seconds; effect outlasts release; task-locked engagement.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Amyotrophic lateral sclerosis: structural and partner changes at C-boutons accompanying motoneuron degeneration.

◦ Spinal injury and motoneuron loss: loss of this gain-control input.

THE ENGINEERING NOTE:

Propose the connection type gain_setting_modulatory_connection.

A message on this connection does not add to the target's input but multiplies the target's responsiveness, carrying a task-demand instruction to amplify.

The nearest artificial-intelligence analogue is a per-unit gain or gating coefficient - a learned multiplier on a neuron's activation - but with the biological difference that the multiplier is set moment to moment by a dedicated modulatory cell through a slow chemical cascade and a specialised calcium-storing organelle, and it is not itself a stored learned parameter.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this synaptic type for the reason that this junction serves a substrate or effector role rather than a cognitive-computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this synaptic type for the reason that this junction serves a substrate or effector role rather than a cognitive-computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A giant cholinergic button backed by its own calcium-storing sac, stamped in rows on a motoneuron's cell body, that never commands a muscle but turns up the volume of every effortful command - the body's built-in turbo switch, from a genetically labelled cluster of Pitx2 cells.


PART FOUR. THE ELECTRICAL AND MIXED JUNCTIONS

Every synapse so far in this catalogue has needed a messenger, a molecule flung across a gap.

The five junction types of this part need none: they join cells by pore or by field, so that current itself is the message.

They are the fastest connections in the nervous system, the only bidirectional ones, and the only ones with no synaptic delay worth the name.

One of them - the interneuron gap junction - earns a seat at cognition's table as the clock fabric of the gamma rhythm.

The rest are substrate: glial welding, unpaired pores, and the strange non-junction where one cell's electrical field simply leans on its neighbour.


CHAPTER 38. THE NEURONAL GAP JUNCTION (THE ELECTRICAL SYNAPSE) (COG)

Somewhere in your cortex right now, a ring of inhibitory interneurons is firing in lockstep at forty times per second, and no chemical messenger is keeping them together.

They are physically continuous: tiny protein tubes run from the inside of one cell straight into the inside of the next, so that a voltage in one is instantly a voltage in all.

This is the electrical synapse, the oldest form of neuronal connection, long dismissed as a primitive leftover and now understood as the metronome of the thinking brain.

It is bidirectional, essentially delay-free, and - the modern surprise - quietly plastic.

Electrical names the currency: no transmitter, only current.

Synapse insists, against a century of chemical chauvinism, that this is a true synapse with its own architecture, its own weight, and its own learning.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT TRANSMITS:

The neuronal gap junction is a cluster of intercellular channels joining the cytoplasm of two neurons, transmitting ionic current and small molecules up to about one kilodalton in mass, a kilodalton being one thousand times the mass of a hydrogen atom.

What crosses is not a spike but a scaled, low-pass-filtered copy of the neighbour's membrane voltage - depolarisations and hyperpolarisations alike, in both directions at once.

THE JUNCTIONAL ARCHITECTURE:

At the junction the two membranes approach to within 2 to 4 nanometres, far closer than the 20 to 25 nanometre cleft of a chemical synapse, a nanometre being a millionth of a millimetre.

Each cell contributes a hemichannel, or connexon, a ring of six connexin protein subunits, and the two hemichannels dock end to end across the narrowed gap to make one continuous aqueous pore.

Tens to hundreds of such channels cluster into a plaque, and ownership is exactly symmetrical: each cell owns precisely half of every channel.

THE MOLECULAR MACHINERY:

The dominant neuronal subunit is connexin 36, encoded by the gene GJD2, with connexin 45 and connexin 30.2 as minor partners in some circuits.

Connexin 36 channels have an unusually low single-channel conductance, around 10 to 15 picosiemens, a picosiemens being a trillionth of a siemens, the unit of electrical conductance.

Strikingly, only a small fraction of the channels in a plaque - on the order of 0.1 to 10 percent - are open at any moment, which leaves enormous headroom for regulation.

The plaque is anchored by scaffolding proteins including zonula occludens 1, and channel open probability is tuned by phosphorylation, the attachment of phosphate groups by kinases such as calcium-calmodulin-dependent protein kinase II.

THE PARTNERS (THE LAYER 6 CAST):

The signature partnership is like with like: parvalbumin basket cells couple to parvalbumin basket cells, somatostatin cells to somatostatin cells, so each inhibitory class forms its own private electrical network.

Other confirmed hosts from the neuron catalogue include the inferior olive's climbing fibre source neurons, thalamic reticular neurons, retinal AII amacrine cells and photoreceptors, hippocampal interneurons, and the mesencephalic trigeminal proprioceptive neurons.

Because both partners are usually the same type, the junction inherits a single personality on both sides - fast, nonadapting cells make fast, precise timing grids.

SIZE AND SCALE:

A plaque spans roughly 0.1 to 0.5 micrometres, a micrometre being a thousandth of a millimetre, holding tens to a few hundred channels.

The functional measure is the coupling coefficient, the fraction of one cell's voltage change seen in its neighbour, typically 0.01 to 0.2 between mammalian interneurons and rarely above 0.5 anywhere.

NUMBER AND DISTRIBUTION:

A cortical parvalbumin interneuron typically couples to 20 to 50 percent of its like-typed neighbours within about 200 micrometres, with each cell carrying a handful to a few dozen junctions.

Whole-brain counts are not established, but electrical synapses are estimated at well under 1 percent of all synapses - a tiny minority with outsised rhythmic authority, moderate confidence.

WHERE IT IS INSTALLED (THE LAYER 9 MAP):

• Neocortex, all areas - coupling within each interneuron class, the fabric of gamma-band synchrony.

• Hippocampus - interneuron networks pacing theta and gamma for memory encoding.

• Thalamic reticular nucleus - coupled inhibitory sheet coordinating sleep spindles.

• Inferior olive - the brain's densest coupling, making the 10 hertz timing carrier for cerebellar learning.

• Retina - AII amacrine and photoreceptor coupling, adjusting spatial pooling with light level.

• Suprachiasmatic nucleus - coupling that sharpens the circadian clock's output.

• Mesencephalic trigeminal nucleus - somatic coupling synchronising jaw proprioceptors.

• Spinal cord and brainstem motor groups - strongest before birth, retained in some adult motor and respiratory networks.

THE TRANSFER FUNCTION:

Output current equals junctional conductance times the voltage difference between the two cells - a linear, ohmic, sign-preserving rule, with junctional conductance typically 0.1 to 1 nanosiemens.

The junction acts as a low-pass filter: slow voltages cross faithfully while brief spikes cross as small blunted bumps called spikelets, so the junction communicates state better than events.

Connexin 36 junctions are essentially nonrectifying, passing current equally in both directions.

TEMPORAL SIGNATURE:

Effective transmission latency is under 0.1 milliseconds, against 0.5 to 1 milliseconds for a chemical synapse, and coupling works down to frequency zero - steady-state voltage is shared indefinitely.

The junction's effective bandwidth is dominated by the neighbour cell's own membrane time constant of 5 to 20 milliseconds, which sets the low-pass corner.

SHORT-TERM DYNAMICS:

There is no vesicle pool to deplete, so classical facilitation and depression are absent.

On the timescale of seconds to minutes, however, calcium entry and magnesium sensitivity can run down or boost coupling - a slow analogue of short-term dynamics called run-down and run-up.

LONG-TERM PLASTICITY:

The quiet revolution: electrical synapses learn.

In the thalamic reticular nucleus and inferior olive, bursts of activity acting through calcium and calcium-calmodulin-dependent protein kinase II produce long-term depression of coupling of 10 to 50 percent lasting tens of minutes or more, and potentiation is also documented.

Because so few channels are open at rest, phosphorylation can in principle swing the weight over a large range without building or removing any channel.

RELIABILITY AND NOISE:

No vesicles means no quantal failures: transmission is continuous and essentially deterministic, with noise limited to channel gating flicker.

The safety factor concept does not apply; what varies across pairs is the coupling coefficient itself, which spans an order of magnitude.

ENERGY BUDGET:

The junction itself is nearly free - no vesicle cycling, no transmitter synthesis - but every ion that crosses must eventually be pumped back by the sodium-potassium adenosine triphosphatase of one cell or the other.

Coupling also loads each cell with its neighbours' conductance, slightly raising the current needed to reach threshold, a distributed cost of belonging to the grid.

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY:

Electrical coupling comes first in life: embryonic and early postnatal networks are broadly coupled, and much of this coupling is pruned as chemical synapses mature, with connexin 36 expression peaking in the second postnatal week in rodents.

Adult interneuron coupling is the deliberate survivor of that pruning, and hemichannels are trafficked, docked, and turned over with half-lives of only a few hours - the plaque is a standing wave, not a fixture.

EVOLUTIONARY PROVENANCE:

Gap junctions are ancient - innexin-based junctions wire invertebrate nervous systems, and the vertebrate connexins are a separate gene family that converged on the same design.

The chemical synapse is the innovation; the electrical synapse is the ancestral condition, retained wherever speed, symmetry, and synchrony pay better than addressable weights.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocyte processes surround coupled dendrites and manage the local potassium that coupling redistributes.

The junction depends on both host cells' kinase and phosphatase balance, which makes it a read-out of neuromodulatory state: dopamine and nitric oxide both dial retinal coupling.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS MICROCIRCUITS:

This junction is the fabric of the interneuron timing grid motifs of the Layer 8 catalogue: the gamma generator, the sleep spindle pacemaker of the thalamic reticular sheet, and the olivary synchrony matrix.

Remove it and each interneuron still inhibits perfectly well, but the population loses its zero-lag agreement: gamma weakens and coarsens, spindles fragment, and the olive's timing carrier decoheres - the circuits keep their brakes and lose their metronome.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS:

Connexin 36 knockout animals show impaired gamma synchrony and altered ocular dominance plasticity; in humans GJD2 variants are a leading association of myopia, through retinal coupling.

Coupling is implicated in seizure synchronisation - gap junction blockers damp some experimental epilepsies - and in the hypersynchrony of parkinsonian circuits.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• SUBSCRIBES (INPUTS).

◦ Neighbour membrane voltage, continuous, minus 90 to plus 30 millivolts.

◦ Modulatory kinase state: calcium-calmodulin-dependent protein kinase II activity, dopamine via protein kinase A.

• PUBLISHES (OUTPUTS).

◦ Junctional current equal to coupling conductance times voltage difference, order 1 to 100 picoamperes; spikelets of 0.1 to 2 millivolts in the neighbour.

• STATE.

◦ The weight: junctional conductance 0.1 to 1 nanosiemens, equivalently coupling coefficient 0.01 to 0.2.

◦ Plasticity variables: fraction of channels open (0.1 to 10 percent), connexin 36 phosphorylation level.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Single-channel conductance 10 to 15 picosiemens; channels per plaque tens to hundreds; membrane separation 2 to 4 nanometres.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Transmission latency under 0.1 milliseconds; low-pass corner set by the 5 to 20 millisecond membrane time constant; plasticity onset minutes, duration tens of minutes to hours; connexin turnover 1 to 5 hours.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ GJD2 variants associate with human myopia; loss of coupling degrades gamma synchrony and spindle coordination; pathological hypersynchrony recruits coupling in epilepsy.

◦ The related connexin 32, in Schwann cells, causes X-linked Charcot-Marie-Tooth disease - a reminder of what broken connexins do to the nervous system.

THE ENGINEERING NOTE:

The proposed connection type is electrical_coupling_connection, whose message is a continuous scaled copy of the sender's voltage rather than a discrete event, delivered symmetrically to both parties.

The nearest artificial intelligence analogue is weight sharing or state averaging between units - a bidirectional ungated edge - but the biological version low-pass filters what it shares and slowly learns how much to share, a trainable averaging coefficient.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to show two neural oscillators, coupled by a gap junction, pulling into synchrony through a coupling coefficient.

Create a number variable called voltage one with initial value minus 70.

Create a number variable called voltage two with initial value minus 50.

Create a number variable called coupling coefficient with initial value 0.15.

Create a number variable called drive one with initial value 1.2.

Create a number variable called drive two with initial value 1.0.

Create a number variable called time step with initial value 0.1.

Repeat the following actions one thousand times.

    Calculate the difference of voltage two and voltage one.

    Multiply the result by the coupling coefficient, and call it junction current into one.

    Calculate the difference of voltage one and voltage two.

    Multiply the result by the coupling coefficient, and call it junction current into two.

    Set voltage one to voltage one plus the time step multiplied by the sum of drive one and junction current into one.

    Set voltage two to voltage two plus the time step multiplied by the sum of drive two and junction current into two.

    If voltage one is greater than minus 40 is true, then perform the following actions.

        Set voltage one to minus 70.

    End the conditional block.

    If voltage two is greater than minus 40 is true, then perform the following actions.

        Set voltage two to minus 70.

    End the conditional block.

    Display voltage one and voltage two to the console.

End the repeated block.

The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because two coupled difference equations and the shrinking gap between their spike times fit on one screen with nothing hidden.

# Two integrate-and-fire oscillators joined by a gap junction.
# Junctional current = g_c * (V_neighbour - V_self); this ohmic, bidirectional
# rule pulls coupled interneurons into near-zero-lag synchrony
# (Connors and Long, Annual Review of Neuroscience 2004).
import math

DT, TAU, V_REST, V_TH, V_RESET = 0.1, 10.0, -70.0, -40.0, -70.0

def run(coupling):
    v1, v2 = -70.0, -55.0            # start out of phase
    drive1, drive2 = 4.0, 4.0
    spikes1, spikes2 = [], []
    for step in range(20000):
        t = step * DT
        j1 = coupling * (v2 - v1)    # current into cell 1 from cell 2
        j2 = coupling * (v1 - v2)    # equal and opposite
        v1 += DT / TAU * (V_REST - v1 + drive1 * TAU / 10 * 10 + j1 * TAU)
        v2 += DT / TAU * (V_REST - v2 + drive2 * TAU / 10 * 10 + j2 * TAU)
        if v1 >= V_TH: v1 = V_RESET; spikes1.append(t)
        if v2 >= V_TH: v2 = V_RESET; spikes2.append(t)
    lags = [min(abs(s - u) for u in spikes2) for s in spikes1[-10:]]
    return sum(lags) / len(lags)

for g in (0.0, 0.05, 0.15):
    print(f"coupling coefficient {g:4.2f} -> mean spike lag {run(g):6.2f} ms")
# Lag collapses toward zero as the coupling coefficient rises:
# the electrical synapse is a synchrony machine.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Under the electron microscope, two membranes running dead straight at 2 to 4 nanometres apart with no vesicles on either side; in the recording rig, a synapse that transmits hyperpolarisation, works in both directions, and has no delay - nothing else in the nervous system does any of those three things, let alone all of them.


CHAPTER 39. THE MIXED CHEMICAL-ELECTRICAL SYNAPSE (NON-COG)

Some junctions refuse to choose.

In one and the same contact, a vesicle-filled active zone sits shoulder to shoulder with a gap junction plaque, so that a single presynaptic spike is delivered twice - once as an instant electrical nudge and once, half a millisecond later, as a chemical pulse.

In fish and frog escape circuits this double delivery is the standard design; in the adult human brain it is rare, contested, and honestly catalogued here as a minor type.

Mixed names the two currencies in one purse.

Chemical-electrical names their order of discovery and, usually, their order of arrival reversed - the electrical component lands first.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT TRANSMITS:

The mixed synapse is a single anatomical contact transmitting both a gap-junctional current and a quantal chemical signal, in mammals apparently always glutamatergic, glutamate being the brain's standard excitatory messenger.

It transmits a fast electrical coupling potential followed by a conventional excitatory postsynaptic potential from the same terminal.

THE JUNCTIONAL ARCHITECTURE:

Within one terminal apposition, patches of 20 to 25 nanometre chemical cleft with vesicles and postsynaptic density alternate with patches of 2 to 4 nanometre gap junction membrane.

The presynaptic cell owns vesicles and hemichannels on its side; the postsynaptic cell owns receptors and the matching hemichannels - the usual split ownership, twice over.

THE MOLECULAR MACHINERY:

The electrical component uses connexin 36 gap junctions at the terminal; the chemical component uses the standard soluble N-ethylmaleimide-sensitive factor attachment protein receptor (SNARE) release machinery, glutamate vesicles, and alpha-amino-3-hydroxy-5-methyl-4-isoxazolepropionic acid (AMPA) receptors.

In mammals, connexin 36 labelling at ultrastructurally identified terminal gap junctions is the decisive evidence, and it has so far been found only at excitatory terminals.

THE PARTNERS (THE LAYER 6 CAST):

The best-documented mammalian sites pair primary afferent or hippocampal glutamatergic axons with brainstem, spinal, and hippocampal principal cells and interneurons.

In the classic fish case - the club endings on the Mauthner cell - the design serves an escape command neuron, which is the evolutionary signature of this type: it appears where microseconds decide survival.

SIZE AND SCALE:

Terminal contacts are conventional in size, 0.5 to 2 micrometres, with gap junction plaques of about 0.1 micrometres embedded in them.

The electrical component is weak, with coupling coefficients usually well under 0.05 in mammals.

NUMBER AND DISTRIBUTION:

In rat and mouse, mixed synapses are reported as abundant in specific brainstem and spinal regions but are a vanishing fraction of synapses overall, and direct demonstration in adult human tissue remains thin - low confidence, honestly stated.

WHERE IT IS INSTALLED (THE LAYER 9 MAP):

• Brainstem motor and vestibular regions - fast startle-adjacent relays where the electrical component trims latency.

• Spinal cord dorsal and ventral horn - primary afferent terminals with terminal gap junctions in rodents.

• Hippocampus - glutamatergic mossy fibre terminals with connexin 36-positive gap junctions reported in adult rat.

• Mesencephalic trigeminal territory - proprioceptive circuitry already rich in coupling.

THE TRANSFER FUNCTION:

The output is the sum of two components: a linear, bidirectional gap-junctional current, and a saturating, unidirectional quantal excitatory conductance of a few hundred picosiemens - a spike arriving produces a fast spikelet riding into a slower excitatory postsynaptic potential.

TEMPORAL SIGNATURE:

The electrical component arrives with essentially zero delay; the chemical component follows after the standard 0.5 to 1 millisecond synaptic delay - a built-in two-beat rhythm within one contact.

SHORT-TERM DYNAMICS:

The chemical half facilitates and depresses like any glutamatergic terminal, while the electrical half holds steady - the junction's reliability floor.

LONG-TERM PLASTICITY:

In goldfish club endings both components show activity-dependent potentiation, and mammalian terminal gap junctions carry the same connexin 36 phosphorylation machinery, but mammalian mixed-synapse plasticity is not established - this junction is catalogued for fidelity, not learning.

RELIABILITY AND NOISE:

The electrical component never fails; the chemical component fails at ordinary quantal rates, so the combined junction has a guaranteed minimum message with a probabilistic bonus.

ENERGY BUDGET:

The chemical half pays the full vesicle-cycle bill; the electrical half adds only pump costs, so the hybrid is barely dearer than a chemical synapse alone.

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY:

Mixed contacts are most numerous early, when widespread developmental coupling overlaps with maturing chemical machinery, and most lose their electrical half with age - the adult residue marks sites where the double message stayed useful.

EVOLUTIONARY PROVENANCE:

This is the transitional form made flesh: the likely evolutionary path from ancestral electrical coupling to addressed chemical transmission, retained wholesale in anamniote escape systems and in traces in mammals.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Standard astrocytic ensheathment serves the chemical half; the electrical half needs only the two partners' kinase balance.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS MICROCIRCUITS:

Where present, it gives fast relay motifs a two-speed input - an instant timing tick plus an integrable weight - and startle-type circuits would lose their latency edge without it.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS:

No human disease is yet pinned to the mixed synapse specifically; its clinical importance rides with the connexin 36 pathologies of Chapter 38 - stated at low confidence because the human inventory itself is uncertain.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• SUBSCRIBES (INPUTS).

◦ Presynaptic spikes; neighbour voltage, continuously, through the junctional patch.

• PUBLISHES (OUTPUTS).

◦ Immediate spikelet of 0.1 to 1 millivolts plus a quantal excitatory postsynaptic potential of 0.1 to 1 millivolts after about 0.5 milliseconds.

• STATE.

◦ Chemical release probability 0.2 to 0.9; junctional conductance order 0.1 nanosiemens.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Chemical cleft 20 to 25 nanometres beside junctional membrane at 2 to 4 nanometres; connexin 36 plus SNARE machinery in one terminal.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Electrical latency under 0.1 milliseconds; chemical latency 0.5 to 1 milliseconds; AMPA decay about 2 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ No dedicated human disease known; loss of the electrical component would silently slow the relay by half a millisecond - a deficit invisible except to circuits that count microseconds.

THE ENGINEERING NOTE:

The proposed connection type is dual_mode_relay_connection, whose message is a guaranteed instantaneous analogue tick bundled with a probabilistic weighted event.

The nearest artificial intelligence analogue is a residual connection summed with a learned weighted edge - identity-plus-weight - though biology runs the two on different clocks and different failure statistics.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this synaptic type for the reason that this junction serves a substrate or effector role rather than a cognitive-computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this synaptic type for the reason that this junction serves a substrate or effector role rather than a cognitive-computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

One terminal, two machines: vesicles queued at an active zone within nanometres of a gap junction plaque, and a postsynaptic response with two rising phases half a millisecond apart.


CHAPTER 40. THE GLIAL GAP JUNCTION PLAQUE (NON-COG)

Neurons couple by the handful of channels; astrocytes couple by the thousands.

Between neighbouring astrocytes lie the largest gap junction plaques in the nervous system, welding hundreds of thousands of cells into one continuous internal space - a syncytium, a many-celled tissue behaving as one cell.

Through it flow potassium, glucose and its breakdown products, calcium-releasing messengers, and water, so that the burden of any busy synapse is shared across millimetres.

Glial names the owners: astrocytes to astrocytes, and astrocytes to oligodendrocytes, the myelin-making cells.

Plaque names the scale - not a channel or two, but a paved field of them.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT TRANSMITS:

The glial gap junction plaque is a dense cluster of intercellular channels joining glial cytoplasms, transmitting ions, metabolites such as glucose and lactate, the calcium-mobilising messenger inositol trisphosphate, and water - a supply and drainage network rather than a signalling wire.

THE JUNCTIONAL ARCHITECTURE:

The geometry is the standard 2 to 4 nanometre gap junction apposition, but scaled up: plaques between astrocyte processes can hold thousands of channels, and each astrocyte forms junctions with many neighbours plus reflexive junctions between its own overlapping processes.

Astrocyte-oligodendrocyte junctions are heterotypic, meaning the two hemichannels are made of different connexins on the two sides.

THE MOLECULAR MACHINERY:

Astrocytes build with connexin 43 (gene GJA1) and connexin 30 (gene GJB6); oligodendrocytes contribute connexin 47 (gene GJC2) and connexin 32 (gene GJB1), docking across the panglial network in specific pairings such as connexin 43 with connexin 47.

Connexin 43 single channels conduct about 50 to 110 picosiemens, several times the neuronal connexin 36, and the plaques pass molecules to about one kilodalton.

THE PARTNERS (THE LAYER 6 CAST):

From the other-cells catalogue: protoplasmic and fibrous astrocytes, interlaminar astrocytes, oligodendrocytes, ependymal cells, and in the periphery the satellite glia of ganglia - a coupling club from which neurons and microglia are excluded.

The astrocyte's territorial personality shapes the network: each cell tiles its own exclusive domain and shakes hands with every neighbour at the borders.

SIZE AND SCALE:

Individual plaques run 0.1 to over 1 micrometre across; a single astrocyte may carry tens of thousands of junctional channels distributed over dozens of plaques.

Dye injected into one human or rodent astrocyte spreads to tens or hundreds of neighbours within minutes - the working definition of the syncytium.

NUMBER AND DISTRIBUTION:

With billions of astrocytes each coupled to most of its neighbours, glial junctional channels outnumber neuronal electrical synapses by orders of magnitude - the largest gap junction system in the body after the heart, moderate confidence.

WHERE IT IS INSTALLED (THE LAYER 9 MAP):

• All grey matter of brain and spinal cord - the potassium and glutamate buffering mesh under every synapse.

• White matter - astrocyte-oligodendrocyte junctions feeding metabolites to myelin and axon.

• Hippocampus - coupled astrocyte fields sustaining the fuel supply for long-term potentiation.

• Retina - Mueller cell and astrocyte coupling for light-evoked potassium siphoning.

• Hypothalamus - coupling that is dynamically dialled during states such as lactation.

• Peripheral ganglia - satellite glial coupling around sensory and autonomic neurons, increased after injury.

THE TRANSFER FUNCTION:

Ohmic and bidirectional for ions, diffusive for metabolites: flux is proportional to the concentration or voltage difference between cells - a linear equaliser whose output is the smoothing of every gradient handed to it.

TEMPORAL SIGNATURE:

Ionic equalisation acts within milliseconds locally; metabolite delivery and calcium wave relay travel at 10 to 25 micrometres per second, crossing millimetres in minutes.

SHORT-TERM DYNAMICS:

This section does not apply to this synaptic type for the reason that there is no event-wise transmission to facilitate or depress; coupling strength changes only on slower regulatory timescales.

LONG-TERM PLASTICITY:

The network does not learn in the synaptic sense, but coupling is regulated: neuronal activity, inflammation, and endothelin signalling change connexin 43 open probability and trafficking over minutes to days - homeostatic adjustment, proudly not memory.

RELIABILITY AND NOISE:

With thousands of parallel channels the plaque is statistically noiseless; the network's redundancy means single-plaque loss is invisible.

ENERGY BUDGET:

The channels are passive, but the network's business - pumping redistributed potassium and shuttling glucose and lactate toward hungry synapses - is a first-order item in the brain's total energy account.

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY:

Astrocyte coupling assembles postnatally as astrocytes tile the tissue, reaching adult density as synaptogenesis peaks - the buffer network is built in step with the load it must carry.

Connexin 43 has one of the fastest turnovers of any membrane protein, with a half-life of 1 to 3 hours, so the syncytium is continuously rebuilt.

EVOLUTIONARY PROVENANCE:

Glial coupling is at least as old as centralised nervous systems, and the panglial syncytium expands with brain size - the human astrocyte network, with its larger and more complex astrocytes, is among the most extensive known.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

This junction IS the support system; its own needs are only the glucose it distributes and the kinase balance that sets channel gating.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS MICROCIRCUITS:

It is the fabric of the glial syncytium motif of the Layer 8 catalogue and the silent partner of every tripartite ensemble: without it, potassium pools at busy synapses, excitability drifts upward, and the fuel line to high-rate circuits thins - epilepsy-adjacent failure by starvation and flooding.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS:

Connexin 32 mutations cause X-linked Charcot-Marie-Tooth disease, a hereditary peripheral neuropathy; connexin 47 mutations cause Pelizaeus-Merzbacher-like hypomyelinating disease; connexin 43 mutations cause oculodentodigital dysplasia; connexin 30 and connexin 26 deafness genes disrupt the cochlea's supporting-cell network; and reactive astrocytes in epilepsy and Alzheimer disease show pathological hemichannel activity.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• SUBSCRIBES (INPUTS).

◦ Local extracellular potassium 3 to 12 millimolar during activity; glucose from capillaries; inositol trisphosphate from neighbouring astrocytes.

• PUBLISHES (OUTPUTS).

◦ Redistributed potassium; lactate and glucose delivered to distant processes; relayed calcium waves at 10 to 25 micrometres per second.

• STATE.

◦ Network coupling strength (dye-coupling extent, tens to hundreds of cells); connexin 43 phosphorylation state.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Connexin 43 single-channel conductance 50 to 110 picosiemens; permeability cut-off near one kilodalton; thousands of channels per plaque.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Ionic equalisation milliseconds; calcium wave transit seconds to minutes; connexin 43 half-life 1 to 3 hours.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Charcot-Marie-Tooth X (connexin 32), Pelizaeus-Merzbacher-like disease (connexin 47), oculodentodigital dysplasia (connexin 43), connexin-related deafness (connexin 26 and 30), and impaired buffering in epileptic foci.

THE ENGINEERING NOTE:

The proposed connection type is syncytial_buffer_connection, whose message is not information but load - ions, fuel, and water flowing down gradients between pooled cells.

The nearest artificial intelligence analogue is parameter averaging across a server pool or shared memory between workers; the biological difference is that nothing is computed here at all - the network exists so that the computing elements elsewhere never see their own waste heat.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this synaptic type for the reason that this junction serves a substrate or effector role rather than a cognitive-computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this synaptic type for the reason that this junction serves a substrate or effector role rather than a cognitive-computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Inject dye into one astrocyte and watch a hundred cells light up: no neuronal junction, and no other junction in this catalogue, turns a population into a single connected interior.


CHAPTER 41. THE PANNEXIN HEMICHANNEL (NON-COG)

Every other entry in this catalogue joins two cells; this one joins a cell to the open street.

The pannexin hemichannel is an unpaired pore - one half of what looks like a gap junction, never docked to a partner - opening from cytoplasm directly into the extracellular space.

Through it the cell publishes adenosine triphosphate and other small molecules to whoever is listening: astrocytes, microglia, immune cells, or the sleep-pressure accounting of the whole brain.

Pannexin names the protein family, cousins in function though not in sequence to the connexins.

Hemichannel names the honest anatomy: half a junction, whole in itself.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT TRANSMITS:

The pannexin hemichannel is a large-pore membrane channel, built mainly from pannexin 1, releasing adenosine triphosphate (ATP), the cell's energy currency doubling as a messenger, together with glutamate and other metabolites up to about one kilodalton, from cytoplasm to extracellular fluid.

Its message is broadcast, unaddressed, and read by purinergic receptors - the receptor family for ATP and its breakdown product adenosine - on any cell in diffusion range.

THE JUNCTIONAL ARCHITECTURE:

There is no cleft and no partner: a heptamer, a ring of seven pannexin 1 subunits, spans one membrane, with its mouth glycosylated so that it cannot dock with a channel on another cell.

The junction's second party is the extracellular space itself, and beyond it the receptors of nearby cells - a one-sided architecture unique in this catalogue.

THE MOLECULAR MACHINERY:

Pannexin 1 (gene PANX1) is near-ubiquitous; pannexin 2 is neuron-enriched and pannexin 3 skeletal.

Opening is driven by strong depolarisation, mechanical stretch, raised extracellular potassium, activation of certain receptors including the N-methyl-D-aspartate (NMDA) glutamate receptor and the purinergic P2X7 receptor, and, terminally, cleavage by caspases - the executioner enzymes of programmed cell death, which lock the channel open to release find-me signals to phagocytes.

THE PARTNERS (THE LAYER 6 CAST):

The sending side includes neurons (notably at postsynaptic sites), astrocytes, oligodendrocytes, microglia, ependymal cells, and endothelium; the receiving side is any purinoceptor-bearing neighbour, with microglia the most avid reader.

SIZE AND SCALE:

The channel passes molecules to roughly one kilodalton through a pore of about 1 nanometre effective diameter, with single-channel conductance reported from tens up to hundreds of picosiemens depending on state.

NUMBER AND DISTRIBUTION:

Expression is broad but channel opening is rare and event-driven - the hemichannel is a mostly-closed emergency and signalling valve, not a standing leak; quantitative densities are not established, low confidence.

WHERE IT IS INSTALLED (THE LAYER 9 MAP):

• Cortex and hippocampus - postsynaptic pannexin 1 shaping excitability and, when overactive, seizure susceptibility.

• Hypothalamus and basal forebrain - ATP-to-adenosine conversion feeding sleep pressure.

• Spinal cord dorsal horn - microglial and neuronal pannexin in neuropathic pain amplification.

• Retina and optic nerve - pressure-sensitive release implicated in ganglion cell stress.

• Carotid body - ATP release in oxygen sensing.

• Vasculature and immune tissue - find-me signal release from dying cells; taste buds use the related channel CALHM1 for the same broadcast logic.

THE TRANSFER FUNCTION:

Input is a permissive state - depolarisation, stretch, receptor drive, or caspase cleavage - and output is efflux of ATP down its steep concentration gradient, roughly a thousandfold from millimolar inside to micromolar or less outside: a thresholded, saturating release valve, sign-neutral in itself, with the downstream sign set by which purinoceptor reads the plume.

TEMPORAL SIGNATURE:

Openings run from tens of milliseconds to sustained minutes in pathology, and the ATP plume acts over micrometres for seconds before ectonucleotidases - enzymes that chew ATP down to adenosine - convert the message into its own slower sequel.

SHORT-TERM DYNAMICS:

The channel inactivates with sustained stimulation and is inhibited by the very ATP it releases - a built-in negative feedback that keeps broadcasts brief.

LONG-TERM PLASTICITY:

This section does not apply to this synaptic type for the reason that an unpaired broadcast valve has no partner-specific weight to strengthen or weaken; regulation is by expression level, not learning.

RELIABILITY AND NOISE:

Release is stochastic and state-dependent by design; there is no failure concept because there is no addressed recipient - the plume either forms or it does not.

ENERGY BUDGET:

Each opening spends the cell's own ATP as the message itself and burdens it with restoring leaked gradients - the only junction in the catalogue whose transmitter is literally the energy currency.

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY:

Pannexin expression peaks in the developing brain, where ATP signalling helps time proliferation and migration, then declines to the adult's watchful baseline.

EVOLUTIONARY PROVENANCE:

Pannexins are the vertebrate descendants of the innexins that form invertebrate gap junctions - the family that once joined cells now, in us, mostly addresses the commons.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Ectonucleotidases on neighbouring membranes are the obligatory co-machinery, turning released ATP into adenosine and thereby writing the second half of every message.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS MICROCIRCUITS:

It supplies the alarm and recruitment channel of the glial motifs: microglial process convergence on injured tissue, astrocyte calcium wave amplification, and the adenosine accumulation that the sleep-pressure motif integrates - circuits would still compute without it, but they would not feel their own damage or fatigue.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS:

Pannexin 1 overactivity is implicated in seizures, migraine, neuropathic pain, and ischaemic neuronal death, where NMDA-receptor-driven pannexin opening deepens excitotoxicity; a human PANX1 loss variant associates with a multisystem disorder including intellectual disability and hearing loss.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• SUBSCRIBES (INPUTS).

◦ Membrane voltage; mechanical stretch; extracellular potassium above about 10 millimolar; NMDA and P2X7 receptor activation; caspase cleavage.

• PUBLISHES (OUTPUTS).

◦ ATP efflux producing local plumes of 0.1 to 10 micromolar; glutamate; find-me signals during apoptosis.

• STATE.

◦ Channel open probability, near zero at rest; surface expression level.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Heptameric pannexin 1; permeability cut-off near one kilodalton; conductance tens to hundreds of picosiemens by state.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Openings tens of milliseconds to minutes; ATP plume lifetime seconds; adenosine sequel minutes to hours.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Overactivity: excitotoxic amplification in stroke, seizure and migraine susceptibility, chronic pain; germline PANX1 loss: syndromic developmental disorder.

THE ENGINEERING NOTE:

The proposed connection type is hemichannel_broadcast_connection, whose message is a local scalar plume - an ATP concentration with no address field.

The nearest artificial intelligence analogue is publishing a value to a shared blackboard or emitting a system-wide interrupt; the biological difference is that the message decays by being eaten, and its decay product is itself the next message.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this synaptic type for the reason that this junction serves a substrate or effector role rather than a cognitive-computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this synaptic type for the reason that this junction serves a substrate or effector role rather than a cognitive-computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Half a gap junction with no partner and no cleft, releasing the cell's own energy currency as a shout to the neighbourhood - the only entry in this catalogue that is a junction with the world rather than with a cell.


CHAPTER 42. THE EPHAPTIC COUPLING SITE (NON-COG)

Strip away the vesicles, the receptors, the pores, even the membrane contact, and one channel of influence remains: the electrical field itself.

Wherever cells stand close enough, in tight enough extracellular quarters, one cell's currents polarise its neighbour with no synaptic structure whatsoever.

Mostly this is background murmur, but in a few places anatomy has amplified it into a deliberate design - most spectacularly the pinceau, the basket cell's paintbrush of axons wrapped around the Purkinje cell's axon initial segment, which inhibits its target in about one tenth of a millisecond.

Ephaptic, from the Greek for touching upon, names influence by proximity rather than connection.

Coupling site, rather than synapse, concedes the anatomy: there is nothing here to see.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT TRANSMITS:

The ephaptic coupling site is a region where the extracellular voltage generated by one cell's currents directly shifts the membrane potential of a neighbour, transmitting a continuous, sign-inverting analogue influence with no transmitter, no channel, and no contact.

Because outward current from a source raises the local extracellular voltage, the neighbour's membrane is effectively hyperpolarised - fast field inhibition is the signature product.

THE JUNCTIONAL ARCHITECTURE:

The architecture is a resistance, not a structure: closely packed membranes with a narrow, high-resistance extracellular space between them, sometimes sealed by glial wrapping.

At the pinceau, dozens of basket cell axon branches interleave around the Purkinje axon initial segment with almost no conventional synapses, forming a field-focusing cage; in olfactory nerve, thousands of unmyelinated axons packed in shared glial sleeves couple side to side.

THE MOLECULAR MACHINERY:

The transmitter inventory is empty by definition; the relevant molecules are the ion channels that generate the source currents - at the pinceau, dense potassium channels of the Kv1 family in the basket cell axons - and the septate-like junctions that seal the compartment's resistance.

THE PARTNERS (THE LAYER 6 CAST):

At the pinceau: the cerebellar basket cell as source and the Purkinje cell axon initial segment as target - the catalogue's most authoritative inhibitor acting at the target's most sensitive point.

Elsewhere: olfactory receptor axons couple to each other; hippocampal and cortical pyramidal populations couple weakly to their own kin through the endogenous field; retinal horizontal cells feed back onto photoreceptor terminals by an ephaptic component within the invaginated triad.

SIZE AND SCALE:

Pinceau field effects reach on the order of 1 millivolt at the target initial segment; population field effects in cortex and hippocampus are 0.1 to 0.5 millivolts per millivolt of field - small numbers applied at exactly the geometry where small numbers decide spikes.

NUMBER AND DISTRIBUTION:

Every Purkinje cell - some 15 million per human cerebellum - wears a pinceau; diffuse ephaptic influence exists wherever packing is tight, which is to say everywhere, but is a designed feature in only a handful of sites, moderate confidence.

WHERE IT IS INSTALLED (THE LAYER 9 MAP):

• Cerebellar cortex - the pinceau, ultra-rapid basket-to-Purkinje inhibition and synchronisation of neighbouring Purkinje cells.

• Olfactory nerve - axon-to-axon coupling synchronising sensory volleys.

• Retina - the horizontal cell feedback component within the photoreceptor triad.

• Hippocampus and neocortex - endogenous field entrainment of population rhythms, strongest during slow oscillations and pathological discharges.

• Vestibular calyx - field and potassium coupling inside the enclosed chalice cleft of the type I hair cell.

• Peripheral nerve, pathologically - crosstalk between demyelinated axons.

THE TRANSFER FUNCTION:

The target's membrane potential shift equals the local extracellular voltage change imposed by the source, scaled by geometry: a linear, instantaneous, sign-inverting function of the source current, with no threshold and no saturation in the physiological range.

At the pinceau a basket cell spike translates to a brief hyperpolarising influence that can delay or veto a Purkinje spike within about 0.1 milliseconds.

TEMPORAL SIGNATURE:

Transmission is effectively instantaneous - faster than any chemical or even electrical synapse, because nothing crosses anything; the influence lasts exactly as long as the source current flows.

SHORT-TERM DYNAMICS:

This section does not apply to this synaptic type for the reason that there is no releasable resource or channel population to facilitate or depress; the coupling is a fixed function of geometry and source current.

LONG-TERM PLASTICITY:

This section does not apply to this synaptic type for the reason that there is no molecular weight to store change in; the coupling changes only if the anatomy or the extracellular resistance changes.

RELIABILITY AND NOISE:

Perfectly reliable and perfectly analogue: every source current produces its scaled effect every time, with noise inherited entirely from the source population.

ENERGY BUDGET:

The coupling itself is free, a by-product of currents already paid for; the pinceau's only dedicated cost is running the basket cell spikes that generate the field.

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY:

The pinceau assembles postnatally as basket axons find the Purkinje initial segment, guided in part by Purkinje cell activity and neurotransmission, with pinceau size patterned into cerebellar zones.

EVOLUTIONARY PROVENANCE:

Field effects are physics, older than biology; evolution's contribution is the amplifier - tight packing, glial sealing, and channel placement that turn an unavoidable murmur into a usable wire, convergently in electric fish and mammalian cerebellum.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Glial ensheathment is the essential resistor: astrocyte and specialised glial wrapping sets the extracellular resistance on which the whole effect depends.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS MICROCIRCUITS:

It gives the cerebellar module motif its fastest brake - inhibition that beats the chemical basket synapse by half a millisecond and synchronises Purkinje neighbours - and gives population rhythms everywhere a weak self-entraining glue; without it the cerebellum's timing edge dulls and oscillations lose a subtle binding force.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS:

In episodic ataxia type 1, mutations of the potassium channel gene KCNA1 abolish basket-to-Purkinje ephaptic inhibition, tying a human movement disorder directly to this junction; runaway field coupling also helps seizures recruit tissue beyond their synaptic reach.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• SUBSCRIBES (INPUTS).

◦ Source cell transmembrane currents, effectively the basket cell spike train at 10 to 150 hertz at the pinceau.

• PUBLISHES (OUTPUTS).

◦ Imposed membrane potential shifts of order 0.1 to 1 millivolts, sign-inverted, at the target initial segment.

• STATE.

◦ None stored; the coupling is memoryless.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Extracellular resistance of the sealed compartment; source-target distance under about 1 micrometre; Kv1 channel density in the pinceau.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Latency about 0.1 milliseconds or less; influence duration equal to source current duration, about 1 millisecond per spike.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Episodic ataxia type 1 (KCNA1): loss of pinceau ephaptic inhibition and cerebellar incoordination; pathological gain of coupling in epileptic and demyelinated tissue.

THE ENGINEERING NOTE:

The proposed connection type is field_influence_connection, whose message is a continuous signed scalar imposed on the target with zero delay and zero storage.

The nearest artificial intelligence analogue is a hard-wired lateral bias term or normalisation signal shared by co-located units; the biological difference is that no one built the wire - the connection is the physics of shared space, sculpted but never instantiated.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this synaptic type for the reason that this junction serves a substrate or effector role rather than a cognitive-computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this synaptic type for the reason that this junction serves a substrate or effector role rather than a cognitive-computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Inhibition with no synapse, no transmitter, no pore, and no delay measurable on a synaptic clock: block every receptor in the dish and the pinceau still silences its Purkinje cell.


PART FIVE. THE PLASTICITY-DEFINED AND LIFE-CYCLE TYPES

A synapse is not born fully weighted, and it does not die abruptly.

The three types of this part are stages in the life of a connection: the pre-built contact that has not yet spoken, the exploratory touch that may become a synapse, and the marked contact being dismantled.

They are types by state rather than by chemistry - the same physical synapse can pass through all three names.

Together they are the write head, the search head, and the erase head of the connectome.


CHAPTER 43. THE SILENT (NMDA-ONLY) SYNAPSE (COG)

The brain keeps a stock of synapses that have never said anything.

Each is structurally complete - terminal, cleft, density, scaffold - but its postsynaptic membrane holds only N-methyl-D-aspartate (NMDA) receptors, which at resting voltage are plugged by magnesium, so glutamate arrives and nothing audible happens.

The synapse is not broken; it is blank tape, waiting for coincidence between its input and its host cell's activity to write the first word - the insertion of alpha-amino-3-hydroxy-5-methyl-4-isoxazolepropionic acid (AMPA) receptors that converts silence into a functional weight.

In development most excitatory synapses begin this way; in the adult, silent synapses persist as the reserve pool from which new learning and, darkly, addiction draw pre-built connections.

Silent names the resting output: exactly zero.

NMDA-only names the mechanism of the silence: a receptor that conducts only when the listener is already depolarised.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT TRANSMITS:

The silent synapse is a glutamatergic contact whose postsynaptic side carries NMDA receptors but no functional AMPA receptors, so it transmits nothing at resting membrane potential and a calcium-rich NMDA current only when presynaptic glutamate coincides with postsynaptic depolarisation.

What it truly transmits, therefore, is not signal but eligibility: the message "this input and this cell were active together," written in calcium.

THE JUNCTIONAL ARCHITECTURE:

Architecturally it is a standard asymmetric spine or shaft synapse - presynaptic terminal with vesicles, cleft of about 20 to 25 nanometres, postsynaptic density - often on a thin or filopodia-like spine, the immature end of the spine spectrum.

The presynaptic cell owns a fully working release site; the postsynaptic cell owns a density scaffold with an empty AMPA slot - the asymmetry of readiness is the type's defining feature.

THE MOLECULAR MACHINERY:

The postsynaptic density holds PSD-95-family scaffolds and NMDA receptors rich in the GluN2B subunit, the slow, calcium-generous subunit of young synapses.

AMPA receptors, mostly GluA1-containing, wait in a reserve pool in the spine and adjacent dendrite, tethered for delivery; unsilencing runs through calcium entering the NMDA receptor, activation of calcium-calmodulin-dependent protein kinase II, and exocytosis and lateral capture of AMPA receptors into transmembrane AMPA-receptor regulatory protein (TARP)-anchored slots.

THE PARTNERS (THE LAYER 6 CAST):

Any glutamatergic pair can hold a silent synapse, but the classic couples are hippocampal CA3 to CA1 pyramidal cells in development, thalamic and cortical inputs to nucleus accumbens medium spiny neurons in addiction models, and inputs onto adult-born dentate granule cells - the catalogue's perpetual newborns.

The presynaptic partner behaves normally and may not know it is talking to a deaf ear; the postsynaptic partner's depolarisation - supplied by its other, speaking synapses - decides when this one gains a voice.

SIZE AND SCALE:

These are small synapses: postsynaptic densities near the bottom of the size range, spine heads under about 0.5 micrometres, NMDA conductance of order 50 picosiemens per receptor with perhaps 5 to 20 receptors.

The functional AMPA conductance is, by definition, zero.

NUMBER AND DISTRIBUTION:

In the first postnatal weeks a large fraction - by some estimates most - of hippocampal excitatory synapses are silent; classical adult estimates were a few percent, but super-resolution imaging in adult mouse visual cortex found about 25 percent of excitatory synapses on layer 5 cells lacking AMPA receptors, concentrated on filopodia - the adult reserve may be far larger than physiology suggested, moderate confidence.

WHERE IT IS INSTALLED (THE LAYER 9 MAP):

• Hippocampus - the canonical site: silent CA3-to-CA1 contacts as the substrate of developmental and adult long-term potentiation.

• Neocortex, all areas - silent contacts in developing sensory maps and a persistent adult reserve on filopodial protrusions.

• Nucleus accumbens - cocaine and other drugs generate new silent synapses whose later unsilencing hardwires craving.

• Dentate gyrus - adult-born granule cells wire in through a silent-first sequence.

• Anterior cingulate cortex - silent synapse recruitment in chronic pain models.

• Visual thalamus and cortex - unsilencing as the mechanism of experience-dependent map refinement.

THE TRANSFER FUNCTION:

At resting potential the transfer function is zero for any input.

When the postsynaptic membrane is depolarised beyond about minus 40 millivolts, magnesium leaves the NMDA pore and the synapse passes a slow calcium-rich current - a coincidence-gated, voltage-dependent function whose output is plasticity rather than drive.

After unsilencing, the synapse acquires the standard linear-then-saturating AMPA transfer function with a quantal conductance step of a few hundred picosiemens.

TEMPORAL SIGNATURE:

The NMDA current rises in a few milliseconds and decays over 50 to 300 milliseconds, GluN2B making the young synapse's window especially long - a wide-open coincidence detector.

Unsilencing itself takes minutes to become measurable and hours to consolidate.

SHORT-TERM DYNAMICS:

Presynaptic facilitation and depression are normal, but they modulate a signal the resting cell cannot hear; short-term dynamics only matter here during depolarised, plasticity-eligible episodes.

LONG-TERM PLASTICITY:

Long-term plasticity is not something this synapse has; it is what this synapse is for.

Coincident activity drives AMPA receptor insertion - unsilencing, the cleanest known expression mechanism of long-term potentiation - while prolonged uncorrelated activity leaves the synapse silent or marks it for elimination by the Chapter 45 pathway.

Unsilencing is bidirectionally revocable: AMPA receptor removal can re-silence a synapse during long-term depression, returning it to the reserve.

RELIABILITY AND NOISE:

Before unsilencing the failure rate at resting potential is 100 percent by design - the textbook signature is transmission failures at minus 70 millivolts with responses appearing at plus 40 millivolts.

After unsilencing the synapse assumes ordinary quantal statistics, with release probability 0.2 to 0.5 and single-fibre responses of a few tens of picoamperes.

ENERGY BUDGET:

The silent synapse is the cheapest synapse in the catalogue to operate - it conducts almost nothing almost always - but its existence is a standing capital cost: the brain pays to build and maintain connections that may never be used, the metabolic price of keeping blank tape on the shelf.

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY:

The silent state is the default first draft: nascent contacts recruit NMDA receptors and scaffold early, AMPA receptors late, so circuit assembly is a wave of unsilencing sweeping through a mostly silent network, timed by experience.

Sensory deprivation stalls the wave - whisker trimming and dark rearing prolong the silent fraction - making this synapse the physical unit of the critical period.

EVOLUTIONARY PROVENANCE:

Wherever NMDA-type coincidence detection exists - which is throughout vertebrates and, in ancestral form, in invertebrates - the silent configuration is available as a state; its systematic use as a developmental staging area is a vertebrate hallmark, and its abundance in adult cortex may scale with a species' reliance on lifelong learning, low confidence on the comparative claim.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes gate unsilencing twice over: their processes regulate glutamate spillover onto the synapse, and astrocyte-secreted factors including glypicans promote AMPA receptor recruitment - the third party of the tripartite ensemble acts here as commissioning engineer.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS MICROCIRCUITS:

The silent synapse is the fabric of every learning motif's expansion budget: the autoassociative memory motif and the cortical map refinement motifs of the Layer 8 catalogue depend on a reservoir of pre-built, unwritten connections that can be committed in minutes without growing an axon.

Without it, learning would wait on structural growth - days instead of minutes - and the pruning loop would have nothing graded to choose among.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS:

In addiction, cocaine and opioids generate silent synapses in the nucleus accumbens whose unsilencing consolidates craving and relapse; in chronic pain, silent synapse recruitment in the anterior cingulate cortex amplifies suffering; and in intellectual disability syndromes such as fragile X, the balance of silent and functional synapses is shifted, leaving circuits immature.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• SUBSCRIBES (INPUTS).

◦ Presynaptic glutamate release, quantal, release probability 0.2 to 0.5.

◦ Postsynaptic membrane potential, gating the magnesium block between minus 70 and minus 40 millivolts.

• PUBLISHES (OUTPUTS).

◦ Before unsilencing: NMDA calcium current only during coincidence, tens of picoamperes at depolarised potentials.

◦ After unsilencing: standard AMPA quantal current of 10 to 30 picoamperes per release.

• STATE.

◦ The weight: functional AMPA conductance, initially exactly zero.

◦ Plasticity variables: reserve AMPA receptor pool size, calcium-calmodulin-dependent protein kinase II activation, spine volume, silent-versus-functional flag.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ NMDA decay 50 to 300 milliseconds (GluN2B-rich); magnesium unblock threshold near minus 40 millivolts; cleft 20 to 25 nanometres.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Coincidence window about 10 to 100 milliseconds; unsilencing onset minutes; consolidation hours; re-silencing possible over similar times.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Pathological unsilencing in addiction (nucleus accumbens) and chronic pain (anterior cingulate cortex); failure to unsilence in critical-period deprivation and in fragile X-type immaturity.

THE ENGINEERING NOTE:

The proposed connection type is silent_eligible_connection, whose message before commitment is only an eligibility event - "sender and receiver coincided" - and after commitment is a standard weighted excitatory event.

The nearest artificial intelligence analogue is a zero-initialised weight with a Hebbian update rule, or a masked connection awaiting unmasking; the biological difference is that the zero weight still owns full hardware and a private coincidence detector, so commitment costs minutes, not growth.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to unsilence a silent synapse: to detect coincidence between presynaptic release and postsynaptic depolarisation through the NMDA receptor, and to insert AMPA receptors so that the contact becomes a functional weight.

Create a number variable called ampa weight with initial value 0.

Create a number variable called reserve pool with initial value 10.

Create a number variable called calcium with initial value 0.

Create a number variable called insertion threshold with initial value 1.5.

Create a number variable called membrane voltage with initial value minus 70.

Repeat the following actions for each time step in the input schedule.

    Read the presynaptic release flag for this time step.

    Read the membrane voltage for this time step.

    If the presynaptic release flag is true and membrane voltage is greater than minus 40 is true, then perform the following actions.

        Add 1 to calcium.

    Otherwise, perform the following actions.

        Multiply calcium by 0.9.

    End the conditional block.

    If calcium is greater than the insertion threshold and reserve pool is greater than 0 is true, then perform the following actions.

        Add 1 to ampa weight.

        Subtract 1 from reserve pool.

    End the conditional block.

    Calculate the product of ampa weight and the presynaptic release flag.

    Display the result to the console as the synaptic current.

End the repeated block.

If ampa weight is greater than 0 is true, then perform the following actions.

    Display the message that the synapse is no longer silent.

End the conditional block.

The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the whole story - zero output, coincidence, insertion, first words - is a short state machine best shown as one legible loop.

# Unsilencing a silent (NMDA-only) synapse.
# At rest the synapse is inaudible: no AMPA receptors, and the NMDA
# receptor is magnesium-blocked. Coincident release plus depolarisation
# admits calcium; enough calcium drives AMPA receptor insertion
# (Isaac, Nicoll and Malenka, Neuron 1995; Liao, Hessler and Malinow,
# Nature 1995).
AMPA_UNIT_PA = 12.0        # picoamperes per inserted receptor batch
INSERTION_THRESHOLD = 1.5  # arbitrary calcium units
DECAY = 0.9

ampa, reserve, calcium = 0, 10, 0.0

# schedule: (release?, postsynaptic voltage in millivolts)
schedule = [(True, -70)] * 5          # lone inputs: silence
schedule += [(True, -30)] * 4         # coincidence: input during depolarisation
schedule += [(True, -70)] * 5         # test again at rest

for step, (release, v) in enumerate(schedule):
    if release and v > -40:           # magnesium leaves the NMDA pore
        calcium += 1.0
    else:
        calcium *= DECAY
    if calcium > INSERTION_THRESHOLD and reserve > 0:
        ampa += 1                     # AMPA receptor batch inserted
        reserve -= 1
    current = ampa * AMPA_UNIT_PA if release else 0.0
    print(f"step {step:2d}  V={v:4d} mV  Ca={calcium:4.1f}  "
          f"AMPA={ampa}  current={current:5.1f} pA")
# The same resting-potential input that produced 0 pA before the
# coincidence now produces a real current: the synapse has been written.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A synapse that fails one hundred percent of the time at resting potential yet answers perfectly at plus 40 millivolts - and that, minutes after a single episode of coincidence, speaks at rest forever after.


CHAPTER 44. THE NASCENT FILOPODIAL CONTACT (NON-COG)

Before a synapse exists, something must reach out.

The dendritic filopodium is that reach: a thin, headless, actin-driven tentacle that extends from a dendrite, gropes through the neuropil for minutes, touches passing axons, and either recruits a synapse or retracts as if the conversation had never happened.

Most touches come to nothing; the few that hold become thin spines, then, sometimes, mushroom spines with a lifetime of decades.

Nascent names the stage: this is wiring under evaluation, not yet wiring.

Filopodial names the organ of search, from the Latin for thread-foot.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT TRANSMITS:

The nascent filopodial contact is a provisional apposition between a dendritic filopodium and an axon, transmitting weak, unreliable glutamatergic test signals - often through N-methyl-D-aspartate (NMDA) receptors only, overlapping the silent state of Chapter 43 - whose real product is a decision: mature or withdraw.

THE JUNCTIONAL ARCHITECTURE:

The filopodium is 1 to over 10 micrometres long and under 0.3 micrometres thick, without a bulbous head, containing actin but almost no scaffold; the contact has at first no postsynaptic density and no aligned active zone - architecture is the outcome of this junction, not its starting condition.

Ownership at this stage is asymmetric in commitment: the axon offers an existing or forming release site, while the dendrite has invested only a disposable protrusion.

THE MOLECULAR MACHINERY:

Actin polymerisation drives the search; adhesion molecules make the handshake - neurexin on the axon binding neuroligin on the dendrite, plus cadherins and the synaptic cell adhesion molecules - and their trans-synaptic grip nucleates scaffold recruitment, PSD-95 arriving over tens of minutes, followed by NMDA and eventually alpha-amino-3-hydroxy-5-methyl-4-isoxazolepropionic acid (AMPA) receptors.

Glutamate itself is a guidance cue: filopodia grow toward active axons, steering by NMDA receptor calcium.

THE PARTNERS (THE LAYER 6 CAST):

Any excitatory axon and any spiny dendrite can meet this way; the density of the process is highest on the young pyramidal neurons of every cortical area, on adult-born granule cells, and - in the adult - near sites of active learning, where filopodia sample the local axonal marketplace.

SIZE AND SCALE:

Contact areas are tiny, well under 0.1 square micrometres, with response amplitudes at or below a few picoamperes when present at all - the smallest and weakest entries in the whole catalogue.

NUMBER AND DISTRIBUTION:

In early development filopodia outnumber spines, with turnover so fast that a young dendrite replaces most of them within hours; in adult cortex filopodia are perhaps 10 to 30 percent of protrusions and are now understood substantially to carry the silent synapses of Chapter 43, moderate confidence.

WHERE IT IS INSTALLED (THE LAYER 9 MAP):

• All developing grey matter - the universal synaptogenic front during the first years of human life.

• Adult neocortex and hippocampus - ongoing filopodial sampling as the structural arm of learning.

• Dentate gyrus - adult-born neurons wiring into mature circuitry by filopodial search.

• Injured or remapping cortex - filopodial density rises where circuits rebuild.

THE TRANSFER FUNCTION:

Electrically, near-null: occasional NMDA-only test currents.

Functionally, a decision function: integrated coincident activity above threshold returns "stabilise" - scaffold assembly and spine conversion - while below threshold returns "retract"; the mathematical character is a noisy accumulate-to-bound choice running over minutes to days.

TEMPORAL SIGNATURE:

Filopodia extend and retract in minutes; a contact that will survive stabilises over hours; conversion to a spine synapse takes hours to days - the slowest transmission decision in the catalogue.

SHORT-TERM DYNAMICS:

This section does not apply to this synaptic type for the reason that transmission is too sparse and unreliable for facilitation or depression to be defined; the meaningful dynamics are structural.

LONG-TERM PLASTICITY:

The contact does not yet have a weight to potentiate; instead it is plasticity in its rawest form - structural selection - and its stabilisation is gated by the same NMDA-calcium signalling that will later govern the mature synapse's learning.

RELIABILITY AND NOISE:

Transmission, when present, is maximally unreliable, and appropriately so: the noise is the exploration - a reliable trial synapse would defeat the purpose of trialling.

ENERGY BUDGET:

Actin treadmilling makes the growing filopodium locally expensive per micrometre, and the strategy as a whole spends heavily on candidates that will be discarded - the metabolic cost of search, paid mostly in development when the brain's energy share is at its lifetime peak.

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY:

This entry IS development and assembly: the filopodial stage is how nearly every spine synapse in this catalogue began, the human cortex running the programme at maximum rate from late gestation through early childhood, producing the synaptic overshoot that Chapter 45's machinery will later trim.

EVOLUTIONARY PROVENANCE:

Filopodial search is generic cell biology - growth cones, immune cells, and fibroblasts all probe with filopodia - recruited by neurons into a synapse-specific audition process; it is conserved wherever spiny neurons exist.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocyte processes physically border the search space and secrete synaptogenic factors - thrombospondins, hevin, glypicans - that raise the local probability of stabilisation; microglia also touch and sometimes seed filopodial growth.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS MICROCIRCUITS:

It is the up escalator of the pruning loop motif and the supply side of every map-formation motif in the Layer 8 catalogue: microcircuits get their candidate edges from this process, and without it the connectome would be frozen - no new edges, only reweighting of the old.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS:

In fragile X syndrome, dendrites carry an excess of long, immature filopodia into adulthood - selection failing to close; in autism spectrum conditions and schizophrenia, the balance of formation and stabilisation is shifted in opposite directions on current evidence, and hypoxic or alcohol-exposed development thins the filopodial front itself.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• SUBSCRIBES (INPUTS).

◦ Local glutamate from candidate axons; adhesion handshakes (neurexin-neuroligin); astrocyte synaptogenic factors.

• PUBLISHES (OUTPUTS).

◦ Occasional NMDA test currents of a few picoamperes; a structural verdict: spine conversion or retraction.

• STATE.

◦ Contact age; adhesion occupancy; accumulated coincidence evidence; actin stability.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Length 1 to 10 micrometres; diameter under 0.3 micrometres; lifetime minutes to days; conversion probability of order 1 in 5 or lower.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Extension and retraction minutes; stabilisation hours; spine conversion hours to days.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Fragile X syndrome: persistent immature filopodia and failed selection; developmental insults that suppress the search front produce under-connected circuits.

THE ENGINEERING NOTE:

The proposed connection type is candidate_edge_connection, whose message is not data but a proposal - "consider an edge here" - with an attached evidence accumulator.

The nearest artificial intelligence analogue is neural architecture search or dynamic edge growth in a graph network; the biological difference is that every candidate edge is physically built at cost before evaluation, and rejection means demolition, not deletion of an index.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this synaptic type for the reason that this junction serves a substrate or effector role rather than a cognitive-computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this synaptic type for the reason that this junction serves a substrate or effector role rather than a cognitive-computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A headless thread of dendrite touching an axon with no density, no head, and almost no current - a synapse identifiable chiefly by the fact that, if you look again tomorrow, it will probably be gone.


CHAPTER 45. THE TAGGED, RETRACTING SYNAPSE (NON-COG)

The connectome is edited by subtraction, and subtraction has an address system.

A weak, underused synapse does not simply fade; it is marked - complement proteins, the immune system's ancient labels for debris and pathogens, are deposited on it - and the mark is read by microglia, which engulf the tagged terminal piece by piece.

Half or more of the synapses a human child builds are removed this way before adulthood, and the same machinery, reawakened, strips synapses in Alzheimer disease and schizophrenia.

Tagged names the molecular address label.

Retracting names the honest state of the structure: a synapse in disassembly, shrinking as its parts are withdrawn and eaten.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT TRANSMITS:

The tagged, retracting synapse is a chemical synapse - most studied in its glutamatergic form - carrying deposited complement proteins and undergoing microglial and astrocytic removal; it still transmits weakly, but its defining signal is outbound in a different channel: an "eat me" message addressed to the brain's immune cells.

THE JUNCTIONAL ARCHITECTURE:

The architecture is a standard synapse in decline: shrunken spine, thinning postsynaptic density, and a presynaptic terminal from which microglia nibble membrane by trogocytosis, the piecemeal biting of living cell membrane, while whole-synapse engulfment also occurs.

A third and fourth party now share the junction: the microglial process reading the tag, and the astrocyte process performing its own quieter phagocytosis through the MEGF10 and MERTK receptor pathways.

THE MOLECULAR MACHINERY:

The tag is the classical complement cascade repurposed: complement component 1q (C1q) binds exposed presynaptic surfaces - phosphatidylserine, the flipped inner-membrane lipid, is one recognised signal - and downstream cleavage deposits the fragment C3b and its derivative iC3b on the synapse.

The reader is the microglial complement receptor 3, a pairing of the proteins CD11b and CD18; the protective counter-label is CD47, a "do not eat me" signal whose presence spares strong synapses.

THE PARTNERS (THE LAYER 6 CAST):

Any neuron pair can find its junction tagged, with the classic documented case being retinal ganglion cell terminals in the visual thalamus; the constant partners are the microglia - the other-cells catalogue's resident immune sentinels - and astrocytes, which together perform the removal.

Activity decides sides: of two converging inputs, the less active is tagged - the junction's fate is set by a competition between its own weight and its neighbours'.

SIZE AND SCALE:

Tagged synapses skew small: complement deposition concentrates on the smallest, weakest terminals and spines, and the retracting structure shrinks further over hours to days until nothing remains but a membrane scar and lysosomal cargo inside a microglial process.

NUMBER AND DISTRIBUTION:

During human childhood and adolescence, on the order of half of cortical excitatory synapses are eliminated - trillions of removals - with the wave peaking in sensory cortex in early childhood and finishing in prefrontal cortex in the third decade; in the healthy adult the process idles at a low maintenance rate, moderate confidence.

WHERE IT IS INSTALLED (THE LAYER 9 MAP):

• Visual thalamus (lateral geniculate nucleus) - the founding case: complement-guided segregation of eye-specific inputs.

• All neocortex - the adolescent pruning wave, latest and heaviest in prefrontal areas.

• Hippocampus - ongoing complement-sensitive turnover in the service of memory updating.

• Cerebellum - climbing fibre surplus elimination in early life, by related activity-dependent competition.

• Neuromuscular junction - polyneuronal innervation trimmed to one axon per fibre in infancy, the peripheral edition of the same subtraction.

THE TRANSFER FUNCTION:

Synaptic transmission continues, weakly and fading; the type's own transfer function is a classification rule: low activity relative to neighbours, plus exposed "eat me" lipids, minus the CD47 shield, maps to a tagging probability - a thresholded competitive function whose output is removal, executed over days.

TEMPORAL SIGNATURE:

Tagging accumulates over hours to days; microglial contact and trogocytosis proceed over tens of minutes per bite; complete removal of a synapse takes roughly a day, and a circuit's pruning wave runs for months to years.

SHORT-TERM DYNAMICS:

This section does not apply to this synaptic type for the reason that its residual transmission is a fading remnant of its original type; the dynamics that define this entry are the kinetics of removal, not of release.

LONG-TERM PLASTICITY:

This junction is long-term depression's endgame made structural: contacts weakened by the learning rules of earlier chapters are the ones the tag finds, so the type embodies the consolidation principle that what is not used is not merely quieted but unbuilt - the down escalator of the connectome, proudly not a learner but the eraser that learning requires.

RELIABILITY AND NOISE:

Removal is probabilistic but strongly biased: strong synapses are rarely tagged and weak ones often spared for a time, giving the circuit statistical rather than surgical subtraction - occasional wrongful removal is the price, and the CD47 shield the appeal process.

ENERGY BUDGET:

Demolition is cheap per synapse compared with operation, and the accounting runs net positive: every removed synapse cancels its standing maintenance and transmission costs, which is one reason cortical energy use per gram falls after childhood.

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY:

This is disassembly as a developmental programme: synaptic overproduction followed by complement-guided elimination is the vertebrate strategy for matching wiring to experience, with human prefrontal cortex pruning into the mid-twenties - the longest construction-by-subtraction project in biology.

EVOLUTIONARY PROVENANCE:

The complement cascade predates nervous systems as an immune labelling scheme for pathogens and debris; its recruitment to sculpt synapses is a vertebrate innovation - the immune system lending the brain its address labels - and the borrowed machinery explains why neuroinflammation so easily tips into wrongful pruning.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:
